<artifact_plan>
id: gen_plan_research_1_idx3
type: research
title: 'Literature Deep-Dive: DLM vs. Phonological Compression'
summary: >-
  Deep literature engagement with Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) and related work to position our null-finding hypothesis
  on phonological density and dependency distance minimization.
runpod_compute_profile: cpu_light
question: >-
  How does our null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relate to, contrast with, and
  jointly constrain the findings of Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) who found that DLM predicts phonological compression?
  What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and
  what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?
research_plan: |-
  ## Research Plan: Deep Literature Engagement on DLM-Phonology Interface

  ### PHASE 1: Primary Target — Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021)

  **Step 1.1: Fetch the arXiv paper**
  - URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900 (abstract page)
  - URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900.pdf (full PDF)
  - Extract: Full methodology section, all tables, all figures, and the conclusion
  - Key questions to answer from the paper:
    a) What exact metric did they use? ("optimality score" vs. raw sum of dependency distances)
    b) How did they measure phonological compression? (phonemes vs. syllables — why did phonemes work but syllables not?)
    c) What was their sample size and language coverage? (how many languages, which families?)
    d) What statistical test did they use? (correlation? regression? what controls?)
    e) What was the direction of their finding? (DLM → phonological compression, i.e., stronger DLM predicts more compression)
    f) Did they test the reverse direction (phonological density → DLM)? If so, what did they find?
    g) What UD treebanks did they use? (version, number of sentences per language)
    h) What was their definition of "phonological compression"? (mean word length in phonemes? total phoneme count per sentence?)

  **Step 1.2: Search for citations and follow-up work**
  - Scholarly search: "Ferrer-i-Cancho Gómez-Rodríguez 2021 dependency distance compression" — find who has cited this work and what they concluded
  - Scholarly search: "dependency distance minimization predicts compression follow-up" — find any replication or extension studies
  - Scholarly search: "DLM phonological compression reverse direction" — find if anyone tested the reverse hypothesis

  ### PHASE 2: Related Work — Phonology-Syntax Interface

  **Step 2.1: Search for work on phonological effects on syntactic structure**
  - Scholarly search: "phonological density syntax optimization cross-linguistic"
  - Scholarly search: "phoneme inventory size dependency distance"
  - Scholarly search: "phonotactic complexity word order typology"
  - General search: "phonological pressure syntactic linearization"

  **Step 2.2: Search for work on cross-level optimization in language**
  - Scholarly search: "cross-level optimization language phonology syntax"
  - Scholarly search: "information rate phonology syntax trade-off"
  - Scholarly search: "channel capacity language structure phonological density"

  ### PHASE 3: Key Related Papers — Verification and Context

  **Step 3.1: Verify and fetch Gerdes (2026, LREC)**
  - Scholarly search: "Gerdes 2026 LREC functional lexical dependency"
  - Search for: "The Grammar Does the Work" Gerdes
  - Extract: How they split functional vs. lexical dependencies, their methodology, and whether they mention phonological factors

  **Step 3.2: Verify and fetch Futrell et al. (2015, PNAS)**
  - Scholarly search: "Futrell 2015 PNAS dependency distance minimization"
  - Extract: Their cross-linguistic MDD findings, language coverage, and whether they discuss phonological factors

  **Step 3.3: Verify and fetch Futrell et al. (2020)**
  - Scholarly search: "Futrell 2020 dependency distance UD treebanks"
  - Extract: Expanded language coverage, any discussion of phonological or modality effects

  **Step 3.4: Verify and fetch Coupé et al. (2019, Science Advances)**
  - Scholarly search: "Coupé 2019 Science Advances information rate languages 39 bits"
  - Extract: Their finding on comparable information rates, methodology for measuring bits/second, and discussion of structural adaptation

  **Step 3.5: Verify and fetch Kaja Dobrovoljc (2026)**
  - Scholarly search: "Kaja Dobrovoljc 2026 corpus choice dependency distance"
  - Search for: "How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates"
  - Extract: Her findings on ordinal ranking instability, corpus-conditioned MDD, and implications for cross-linguistic comparisons

  **Step 3.6: Verify and fetch Ferrer-i-Cancho (2021) — separate paper**
  - Scholarly search: "Ferrer-i-Cancho 2021 dependency distance minimization predicts compression"
  - Extract: Any additional findings beyond the joint paper with Gómez-Rodríguez

  **Step 3.7: Verify and fetch Krielke (2024)**
  - Scholarly search: "Krielke 2024 diachronic dependency relations scientific English German"
  - Extract: Diachronic shift findings and any mention of phonological-density interaction

  ### PHASE 4: Broader Context — DLM Literature

  **Step 4.1: Survey the DLM literature landscape**
  - Scholarly search: "dependency distance minimization cross-linguistic survey 2020-2026"
  - Scholarly search: "mean dependency distance typology word order"
  - Scholarly search: "dependency distance spoken written modality"

  **Step 4.2: Search for work on spoken vs. written DLM**
  - Scholarly search: "dependency distance spoken language vs written language"
  - Scholarly search: "DLM modality effect speech writing"
  - Scholarly search: "spoken corpus dependency parsing UD"

  **Step 4.3: Search for phylogenetic control methods in cross-linguistic studies**
  - Scholarly search: "phylogenetic control cross-linguistic regression language family"
  - Scholarly search: "mixed-effects model language family random effect typology"
  - Scholarly search: "phylogenetic independent contrasts linguistic typology"

  ### PHASE 5: Synthesis and Report Writing

  **Step 5.1: Create a structured comparison table**
  - Build a table comparing Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) with our hypothesis:
    - Direction of prediction (forward vs. reverse)
    - Unit of analysis (words vs. phonemes)
    - Statistical approach
    - Language coverage
    - Key findings
    - Limitations identified

  **Step 5.2: Map the conceptual relationship**
  - Write a clear explanation of how the two studies' findings relate:
    a) If A→B (DLM predicts compression) is true, does B→A (compression predicts DLM) have to be true? (correlation asymmetry)
    b) What does our null finding (B does NOT predict A) add to their positive finding (A predicts B)?
    c) Could both be simultaneously true under a directional/causal interpretation?
    d) What does this imply for theories of cross-level optimization?

  **Step 5.3: Identify the novel contribution**
  - Articulate what our null finding adds that was not known before:
    a) The independence of phonological density and syntactic optimization
    b) The asymmetry between forward and reverse predictions
    c) The need for alternative mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic mediation)
    d) The methodological contribution of phoneme-space distance metrics

  **Step 5.4: Compile corrected citations**
  - For each paper, compile:
    - Full author list
    - Exact title
    - Venue and year
    - DOI or arXiv ID
    - Key finding (1 sentence)
    - Relevance to our study (1 sentence)

  **Step 5.5: Write the research report**
  - Structure the report as:
    1. Executive Summary (2-3 paragraphs)
    2. Deep Dive: Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) (methodology, findings, limitations)
    3. The Reverse-Direction Question (our hypothesis vs. their finding)
    4. Related Work Survey (Gerdes, Futrell, Coupé, Dobrovoljc, Krielke)
    5. Conceptual Synthesis (what the combination of findings implies)
    6. Novel Contribution Statement (what our study adds)
    7. Corrected Citation Table
    8. Open Questions and Follow-up Directions

  ### OUTPUT FORMAT

  **research_out.json:**
  ```json
  {
    "answer": "<comprehensive answer to the research question>",
    "sources": [
      {"title": "...", "url": "...", "key_finding": "..."}
    ],
    "follow_up_questions": [
      "<question 1>",
      "<question 2>"
    ]
  }
  ```

  **research_report.md:**
  A full markdown report following the structure in Step 5.5 above, with in-text citations and a reference list.

  ### BUDGET AND PRIORITY GUIDANCE

  - **Budget**: $10 USD for OpenRouter API calls. Prioritize:
    1. Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) paper (MUST fetch and analyze deeply)
    2. Dobrovoljc (2026) paper (important for reviewer calibration)
    3. Gerdes (2026) paper (functional vs. lexical split)
    4. Futrell et al. (2015, 2020) papers (foundational DLM work)
    5. Coupé et al. (2019) (information rate context)
    6. Broader literature search (only if budget allows)

  - **Priority**: The single most important output is a precise understanding of Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) methodology and findings, because everything else hinges on correctly positioning our null finding against their positive finding.

  - **Failure mode**: If the arXiv PDF cannot be fetched, fall back to the abstract page and to citing papers that summarize their methodology. If scholarly search returns too few results, expand to general web search with arXiv-specific queries.

  ### CRITICAL SUCCESS CRITERIA FOR THIS RESEARCH

  The research is successful if the executor produces:
  1. A precise summary of Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021) methodology (metric, sample, test, finding)
  2. A clear articulation of how our reverse-direction null finding relates to their forward-direction positive finding
  3. Corrected citations for all 8+ key papers with DOIs/arXiv IDs
  4. A synthesized "novel contribution" statement that the paper writer can use directly
  5. Identification of 3-5 specific open questions that emerge from the literature gap
explanation: >-
  This research is critical because our hypothesis makes a null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength)
  that stands in tension with Ferrer-i-Cancho & Gómez-Rodríguez (2021), who found that DLM predicts phonological compression.
  We must precisely understand their methodology, findings, and limitations to correctly position our result. Without this
  deep engagement, our paper risks being rejected for failing to engage with the most directly relevant prior work. The research
  will produce a structured report that the paper writer can use to craft a compelling related work section and a clear novel
  contribution statement. This is especially important given that our reviewer (Kaja Dobrovoljc) has published work on corpus-conditioned
  dependency distance estimates, so the literature engagement must be thorough and precise.
</artifact_plan>

<investigation_process>

5. CONTRAST: Actively try to disprove your emerging conclusions. Search with different phrasings, "[topic] criticism", "[topic] limitations". Check across fields — the same finding may exist under different names
6. SYNTHESIZE: Integrate into balanced conclusion
7. ITERATE: Expect to repeat steps 2-6 if findings are incomplete or one-sided. Don't settle on first results
8. SUMMARIZE: Output JSON must include 'title' and 'summary' fields
</investigation_process>

### [4] HUMAN-USER prompt · 2026-09-08 00:13:52 UTC

```
Direction: Computational Linguistics — Dependency Distance Minimization Across UD Treebanks. Something genuinely novel and groundbreaking that measures dependency-distance distributions across UD treebanks, investigates whether spoken language minimizes more than written, characterizes how typology interacts with the pattern, and identifies families that deviate. MUST use commul/universal_dependencies on HuggingFace.

Ambition: level 3 of 5 — phenomenological science: surface and rigorously characterize a new empirical regularity or anomaly in the data, even before a full theoretical explanation exists.

Reviewer: I am Kaja Dobrovoljc (JSI / University of Ljubljana). Calibrate from my existing papers. Cross-domain methods (information theory, mixed-effects models, sequence models) welcome — but keep them close to what I already know, not too far from my background.

Submission/Goal: a paper for ACL, EMNLP, or the Computational Linguistics journal. Audience: computational linguists and quantitative typologists. Tone: empirically rigorous, careful with linguistic detail, reproducible on public UD.
```

{"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "## Web tools\n\nYou have three web capabilities: **search**, **fetch**, and **grep** (exact\nregex extraction over a full page or PDF).\n\n**Pick where they come from, in this order:**\n\n1. **If you have built-in `WebSearch` / `WebFetch` tools, PREFER those over the\n   scripts below.** They may be **deferred tools** (listed by name but with\n   schemas not yet loaded) \u2014 if so, call `ToolSearch(\"select:WebSearch,WebFetch\")`\n   ONCE to load them, then use them normally. Do not skip them just because they\n   need that one extra load step; they are the preferred path. Pair them with the\n   `aii_web_tools__fetch_grep` script below when you need exact text / numbers /\n   methodology that a summary would miss, or when reading a PDF.\n2. **Only if you have NO built-in `WebSearch` / `WebFetch`** (e.g. the OpenHands\n   backend), use the scripts in this skill (below). They are our own\n   implementations \u2014 free-first web search (keyless general/scholarly engines,\n   Serper fallback), html2text + PyMuPDF for fetch, and regex grep over the full\n   document text. They work without any built-in web tools.\n\nWorkflow either way: **search** (discover) \u2192 **fetch** (read for the gist) \u2192\n**grep** (pull exact details / read PDFs).\n\n---\n\n## Running the scripts\n\nRun every script with the skill's pre-provisioned interpreter (it already has\n`requests`, `html2text`, `pymupdf`, `python-dotenv`). Set `PY` once:\n\n```bash\nexport SKILL_DIR=\"$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel 2>/dev/null || echo /ai-inventor)/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools\"\nexport PY=\"$SKILL_DIR/../.ability_client_venv/bin/python\"\n```\n\n### 1. Search the web (free-first: general or scholarly)\n\n```bash\n# general web (default): keyless engines (ddgs, marginalia); Serper only if they miss\n$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_search.py\" --query \"neuro-symbolic FOL translation LLM\" --max-results 10\n# scholarly mode: OpenAlex + Crossref (DOIs, citation counts)\n$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_search.py\" --query \"neuro-symbolic FOL translation\" --mode scholarly\n```\n\nReturns ranked title / URL / snippet lines. `--mode general` (default) uses\nkeyless general engines; `--mode scholarly` uses academic APIs. Both fall back\nto Serper (paid) only when the free engines miss. Use search first to scan the\nlandscape; snippets are for discovery only \u2014 fetch a page before judging it.\n\n### 2. Fetch a page as markdown (HTML or PDF)\n\n```bash\n$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" fetch --url \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11366\" --max-chars 10000\n```\n\n`--max-chars` caps output (default 10000); `--char-offset N` pages further in.\nHandles PDFs transparently via PyMuPDF.\n\n### 3. Grep a page or PDF (exact regex extraction)\n\n```bash\n$PY \"$SKILL_DIR/scripts/aii_fast_web_fetch.py\" grep --url \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.11366\" --pattern \"verbal reinforcement\" --max-matches 20 --context-chars 200\n```\n\nReturns only the matching sections with surrounding context \u2014 the right tool\nfor exact numbers, table values, methodology, or long PDFs where a summary\nwould lose the detail. `-i` for case-insensitive.\n\n**Parallelize** independent searches/fetches in one turn; only sequence a\nfetch after the search that produced its URL.\n\n---\n\n## Notes\n\n- The scripts call our ability server. If a script prints\n  `Ability service not available`, the server is down \u2014 say so rather than\n  silently improvising a different search method.\n- Do **not** hand-roll your own `requests`/scraping for search when these\n  tools are available: Serper returns clean Google results and the fetch/grep\n  scripts already handle HTML, PDFs, and encoding.\n\n---\nThis skill is located at `/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools`. prompting interventions \"partially reduce this bias but trigger a calibration\n  crisis\" [S8] (2026).\n\n**Cognitive modelling and the scale paradox**\n\n- Surprisal's fit to reading times peaks near two billion training tokens, after which perplexity\n  gains produce \"poorer fits to human reading times\" [S16] (Findings of EMNLP 2023).\n- Not an artifact of latency measures \u2014 the inverse relation \"still obtains\" on two fMRI datasets\n  across 17 LMs [S17] (EACL 2026).\n- The predictor is unstable: early-layer representations beat surprisal on early-pass eye movements,\n  and \"the best-performing predictor varies strongly depending on the language and eye-tracking\n  measure\" [S23] (ACL 2026).\n\n**The resource map and language varieties**\n\n- Catalogue counts mislead: 59% of the surveyed languages score zero catalogued-dataset density, yet literature mining shows active dataset production for many [S5] (2026-05).\n- The dominant multilingual benchmark is criticized at protocol level \u2014 many translations \"fall below\n  the claimed 90% quality standard\", and \"copying named entities, can yield non-trivial BLEU\n  scores\" [S13] (EMNLP 2025).\n\n**Annotation, disagreement, pluralism**\n\n- The perspectivist turn has its own survey, mapping \"a shift from consensus learning toward\n  explicitly modeling disagreement, and toward capturing structured relationships among\n  annotators\" [S6] (2026-01).\n\n**Meta-science of the field**\n\n- Submission volume outran reviewing capacity \u2014 17,087 submissions against 1,424 area chairs,\n  with the community weighing \"options for limiting submissions for the first time in ACL's\n  history\" [S1] (2026-05) \u2014 yet measured review quality shows \"no consistent decline in median\n  review quality across venues and years\" [S22] (2026-01). They are our own\n   implementations \u2014 free-first web search (keyless general/scholarly engines,\n   Serper fallback), html2text + PyMuPDF for fetch, and regex grep over the full\n   document text. They work without any built-in web tools.\n\nWorkflow either way: **search** (discover) \u2192 **fetch** (read for the gist) \u2192\n**grep** (pull exact details / read PDFs).\n\n---\n\n## Running the scripts\n\nRun every script with the skill's pre-provisioned interpreter (it already has\n`requests`, `html2text`, `pymupdf`, `python-dotenv`). Set `PY` once:\n\n```bash\nexport SKILL_DIR=\"$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel 2>/dev/null || echo /ai-inventor)/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools\"\nexport PY=\"$SKILL_DIR/../.ability_client_venv/bin/python\"\n```\n\n### 1. {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900\nType: HTML\nLength: 8407 chars\n\n--- Content ---\n\nSkip to main content [](https://arxiv.org/IgnoreMe) [ ](https://arxiv.org/) [ Search ](https://arxiv.org/search) [Submit](https://arxiv.org/user/create) [Donate](https://info.arxiv.org/about/donate.html) [Log in](https://arxiv.org/login)\n\nSearch arXiv\n\nPress Enter to search * [Advanced search](https://arxiv.org/search/advanced)\n\n# Computer Science > Computation and Language\n\n**arXiv:2109.08900** (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 18 Sep 2021 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900v1)), last revised 4 Dec 2021 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\n\nAuthors:[Ramon Ferrer-i-Cancho](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Ferrer-i-Cancho,+R), [Carlos G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=G%C3%B3mez-Rodr%C3%ADguez,+C)\n\nView a PDF of the paper titled Dependency distance minimization predicts compression, by Ramon Ferrer-i-Cancho and Carlos G\\'omez-Rodr\\'iguez\n\n[View PDF](/pdf/2109.08900) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2109.08900v2)\n\n> Abstract:Dependency distance minimization (DDm) is a well-established principle of word order. It has been predicted theoretically that DDm implies compression, namely the minimization of word lengths. This is a second order prediction because it links a principle with another principle, rather than a principle and a manifestation as in a first order prediction. Here we test that second order prediction with a parallel collection of treebanks controlling for annotation style with Universal Dependencies and Surface-Syntactic Universal Dependencies. To test it, we use a recently introduced score that has many mathematical and statistical advantages with respect to the widely used sum of dependency distances. We find that the prediction is confirmed by the new score when word lengths are measured in phonemes, independently of the annotation style, but not when word lengths are measured in syllables. In contrast, one of the most widely used scores, i.e. the sum of dependency distances, fails to confirm that prediction, showing the weakness of raw dependency distances for research on word order. Finally, our findings expand the theory of natural communication by linking two distinct levels of organization, namely syntax (word order) and word internal structure.\n\nComments: | To appear in the proceedings of the SyntaFest 2022 (Sofia, Bulgaria, March 21-25)\n---|---\nSubjects: |  Computation and Language (cs.CL); Neurons and Cognition (q-bio.NC)\nCite as: | {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\nType: PDF\nLength: 41818 chars\n\n--- Content ---\n\nDependency distance minimization predicts compression\nRamon Ferrer-i-Cancho\nComplexity and Quantitative Linguistics Lab,\nLARCA Research Group\nDepartament de Ci`encies de la Computaci\u00b4o\nUniversitat Polit`ecnica de Catalunya\nCampus Nord, Edi\ufb01ci Omega\nJordi Girona Salgado 1-3\n08034 Barcelona, Catalonia, Spain\nrferrericancho@cs.upc.edu\nCarlos G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez\nUniversidade da Coru\u02dcna, CITIC\nFASTPARSE Lab, LyS Research Group\nDepartamento de Ciencias\nde la Computaci\u00b4on y Tecnolog\u00b4\u0131as\nde la Informaci\u00b4on\nFacultade de Inform\u00b4atica, Elvi\u02dcna,\n15071, A Coru\u02dcna, Spain\ncgomezr@udc.es\nAbstract\nDependency distance minimization (DDm) is a well-established principle of word order. It has\nbeen predicted theoretically that DDm implies compression, namely the minimization of word\nlengths. This is a second order prediction because it links a principle with another principle,\nrather than a principle and a manifestation as in a \ufb01rst order prediction. Here we test that sec-\nond order prediction with a parallel collection of treebanks controlling for annotation style with\nUniversal Dependencies and Surface-Syntactic Universal Dependencies. To test it, we use a re-\ncently introduced score that has many mathematical and statistical advantages with respect to the\nwidely used sum of dependency distances. We \ufb01nd that the prediction is con\ufb01rmed by the new\nscore when word lengths are measured in phonemes, independently of the annotation style, but\nnot when word lengths are measured in syllables. In contrast, one of the most widely used scores,\ni.e. the sum of dependency distances, fails to con\ufb01rm that prediction, showing the weakness of\nraw dependency distances for research on word order. Finally, our \ufb01ndings expand the theory\nof natural communication by linking two distinct levels of organization, namely syntax (word\norder) and word internal structure.\n1\nIntroduction\nAccording to the dependency distance minimization (DDm) principle, the distance between heads and\ntheir dependent words in a sentence has to be reduced (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2004; Gildea and Temperley,\n2007). The principle has been supported widely by studies whose coverage of languages and families\nis increasing over time (e.g., Liu (2008), Futrell et al. (2015), Futrell et al. (2020), Ferrer-i-Cancho et\nal. (2021)). For simplicity, such distance is usually measured in words (see Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b)\nfor an exception) but it could be measured with more precision in syllables or phonemes (Ferrer-i-\nCancho, 2015b). In that way, the distance of a dependency would be a function of the length of the\nwords de\ufb01ning the dependency and that of the words in-between. For this reason, it was predicted that\nword lengths should be minimized to minimize dependency distances (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2017a; Ferrer-\ni-Cancho, 2017b), namely the DDm principle predicts compression, i.e., another principle whereby L,\nthe mean word length, has to be minimized. However, to our knowledge, such a prediction has never\nbeen tested in spite of its great theoretical importance. First, it is crucial for the construction of a the-\nory of language and other natural communication systems (Semple et al., 2021). A critical component\nof a theory are the predictions that it can make. For instance, DDm predicts the scarcity of crossing\ndependencies (G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez and Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2017) and compression of word lengths predicts\nZipf\u2019s law of abbreviation (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2019). These are examples of \ufb01rst order predictions,\nnamely manifestations that are predicted by a certain principle. The focus of this article are second order\npredictions, namely, principles that are predicted by other principles, such as (a) the prediction of DDm\nfrom Dm, a general principle of distance minimization (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021; Ferrer-i-Cancho,\n2003) in the spirit of Behaghel\u2019s pioneering views (Behaghel, 1932), or (b) a principle of surprisal or\nentropy minimization (Hm) from compression (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2018). Table 1 summarizes these and\narXiv:2109.08900v2  [cs.CL]  4 Dec 2021\n\nother \ufb01rst order and second order predictions from previous research stemming from a general principle\nof energy minimization (Em) in the spirit of Zipf\u2019s least effort principle (Zipf, 1949) but extended to\nother biological systems (Semple et al., 2021).\nIn the quantitative linguistics tradition, syllables are considered to be one of the best units (if not the\nbest one) for measuring word length, mainly to warrant cross-linguistic validity (Popescu et al., 2013;\nGrzybek, 2013). In addition, phonemes (and graphemes) are considered to not be appropriate because\nthey are not immediate constituents of words (words are made of syllables that are made in turn of\nphonemes). Here we will revise this view arising from research on word lengths and check if it still\napplies for research on the interaction between dependency distance (in words) and word length.\nThis article is aimed at testing the hypothesis that DDm implies compression, which we refer to as\nrepertoire unit weight minimization (RUWm). We follow the convention that a lower case m at the\nend of the abbreviation of a principle indicates \u201dminimization\u201d (Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez,\n2021). RUWm is the minimization of the weight (or cost) of units in a repertoire. In the context of\nword types and their length as their weight, RUWm corresponds to the minimization of word lengths.\nRUWm, predicts that more likely units in a repertoire (e.g., more frequent words in a vocabulary) should\nbe lighter (e.g., be shorter). In particular, RUWm has been argued to lead to the emergence of the law of\nabbreviation across linguistic levels (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2019): e.g., the lexical level where frequent\nword types or meanings tend to have shorter forms (Zipf, 1949; Kanwal et al., 2017; Brochhagen, 2021),\nand the sublexical level where more frequent cases or case marker types tend to have shorter forms (Liu,\n2021). RUWm is a specialization of a more general principle of unit weight minimization (UWm) arising\nfrom research on unifying compression with the origins of both the law of abbreviation and Menzerath\u2019s\nlaw (Gustison et al., 2016). The law of abbreviation is the tendency of more frequent words to be\nshorter (Zipf, 1949) while Menzerath\u2019s law is the tendency of linguistic constructs with more parts to be\nmade of smaller parts (Altmann, 1980). A specialization of UWm on sequences, sequence unit weight\nminimization (SUWm) sheds light on the possible origin of Menzerath\u2019s law (Gustison et al. (2016);\nTable 1). In the context of words as sequences of syllables and the length of as syllable as its weight\n(or cost), SUWm corresponds to the minimization of the lengths of syllables in the words they appear.\nSUWm predicts that units in longer sequences (e.g., syllables in words with more syllables) should have\nsmaller weight (e.g., be shorter).\nFormally, we aim to test\nDDm \u2212\u2192RUWm.\n(1)\nAs an alternative to that prediction one could consider a compensation hypothesis, where less optimized\nlanguages at the level of dependency distances would have more pressure for shorter words (or more\noptimized languages at the level of dependency distances would tolerate longer words). The idea of\ncompensation has been applied in word order research in different ways (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2018; Ferrer-\ni-Cancho, 2015b). For instance, the suboptimal placement of the verb in SOV orders with respect to\nDDm (DDm predicts SVO or OVS) has been argued to be compensated by the short length of clitics\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2015b).\n2\nMethods\nTo measure dependency distance in a sentence, we considered two scores, both computed using depen-\ndency distances measured in words. The \ufb01rst score is \u2126, a recently introduced normalized score that\ntakes the value 1 when the dependency distances are fully optimized and is expected to take a value of 0\nif there is no bias on dependency distances, for or against DDm (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021). \u2126can be\nseen as score of the intensity of DDm, which is maximum when \u2126= 1, and missing when \u2126\u22480. \u2126< 0\nindicates that DDm is surpassed by other word order principles. The score has the virtue of satisfying\na series of mathematical and statistical properties: dual normalization (i.e. normalization with respect\nto both the minimum and the random baseline), constancy under minimum linear arrangement, stability\nunder random linear arrangement, invariance under linear transformation and boundedness under max-\nimum linear arrangement (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021). These properties are particularly useful when\ncalculating an average score over the sentences of a treebank. For comparison, we consider also D, the\n\nEm\n\u2212\u2192\nDm\n\u2212\u2192\nSDm\n\u2212\u2192\npairs of primarily alternating word orders (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2016)\n\u2212\u2192\nDDm\n\u2212\u2192\nacceptability (Morrill, 2000)\n\u2212\u2192\nword order preferences (Morrill, 2000)\n\u2212\u2192\nscarcity of crossing dependencies\n(G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez and Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2017)\n\u2212\u2192\ntendency to uncover the root (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2008)\n\u2212\u2192\nprojectivity & planarity with high probability\nDDm (+ projectivity)\n\u2212\u2192\nmedial placement of the root\n(Gildea and Temperley, 2007; Alemany-Puig et al., 2022)\nDDm (+ planarity)\n\u2212\u2192\nmedial placement of the central vertex\n(Iordanskii, 1987; Hochberg and Stallmann, 2003; Alemany-Puig et al., 2022)\nDDm + extreme V placement\n\u2212\u2192\nplacement of adjectives with respect to nominal heads\nin SVO triples\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2008; Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2015a)\n\u2212\u2192\nplacement of auxiliary V with respect to main V\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2008)\n\u2212\u2192\nconsistent branching for dependents of nominal heads\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2015b)\n\u2212\u2192\n\u201dunnecessity\u201d of headedness parameter\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2015b)\n\u2212\u2192\nRUWm (to be tested in this article)\n\u2212\u2192\nUWm\n\u2212\u2192\nRUWm\n\u2212\u2192\nZipf\u2019s law of abbreviation\n(Shannon, 1948; Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2019)\nreduction\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2017a)\nRUWm +\n\u2212\u2192\nHm (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2018)\nunique segmentation\nSUWm\n\u2212\u2192\nMenzerath\u2019s law\n(Gustison et al., 2016; Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2019)\nTable 1: Optimization principles and their predictions. Arrows link principles with their predictions. Predictions can take the form of principles or manifestations.\nPrinciples are marked in boldface. The two principles whose relationship is the target of this article are marked in blue. Em: energy minimization. Dm: distance\nminimization. UWm: unit weight minimization, popularly known as compression. RUWm: repertoire unit weight minimization. SUWm: sequence unit weight\nminimization. SDm: swap distance minimization. DDm: dependency distance minimization. Hm: entropy (or surprisal) minimization. We use parentheses for\nassumptions that are likely to be predictions of DDm and thus likely to be unnecessary assumptions to a large extent (see G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez and Ferrer-i-Cancho\n(2017) for further details about the argument). Awareness of such unnecessary assumptions is vital for the construction of a parsimonious theory.\n\nsum of dependencies of a sentence. While \u2126is a measure of closeness, D is a measure of distance. D\nis the most widely used score (Gildea and Temperley, 2007; Gildea and Temperley, 2010; Futrell et al.,\n2015; Futrell et al., 2020) but does not satisfy any of the remarkable mathematical properties enumerated\nabove. The intensity of DDm is dif\ufb01cult to assess just from the value of D.\nFor each treebank considered in this study, we calculated an average \u2126and average D over all the\nsentences of the treebank. To measure word length, we considered two different units: phonemes and\nsyllables. For each language, we calculated Ls, the mean word length in syllables (mean syllables per\nword token) and Lp, the mean word length in phonemes (mean phonemes per word token).\nTo control for the content or the source text of the treebanks, we used a parallel collection of tree-\nbanks, in particular, the Parallel Universal Dependencies (PUD) collection version 2.6 (Zeman et al.,\n2017). PUD contains 20 languages from 9 distinct families. PUD follows the UD annotation style (Ze-\nman et al., 2020). To control for annotation style, we also use SUD, i.e. Surface-Syntactic Universal\nDependencies (Gerdes et al., 2018). We use PSUD to refer to the PUD collection following the SUD\nannotation (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021). The PUD and PSUD treebank collections are borrowed from\na recent study (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021). The preprocessing of these treebanks involves the removal\nof punctuation marks and reparalellization to warrant there is no loss of parallelism after punctuation\nmark removal (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021). The data are available from https://github.com/\nlluisalemanypuig/optimality-syntactic-dependency-distances in two levels of\npreprocessing: (a) the preprocessed treebanks as head vectors and (b) the raw text tables that were ex-\ntracted from them and used to feed the statistical analyses. The transformation of the raw head vectors\ninto the raw text tables can be replicated easily with the Linear Arrangement Library (Alemany-Puig and\nFerrer-i-Cancho, 2022).\nThe PUD data does not include syllable or phoneme annotations that would allow us to measure word\nlengths in these units, and obtaining them would be highly costly. Thus, we instead borrowed mean\nword lengths from the dataset of another recent study (Fenk-Oczlon and Pilz, 2021). In that study,\nmean word lengths were estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences encoding one proposition and\nusing basic vocabulary. Three languages from PUD/PSUD are missing in that dataset: Arabic (Afro-\nAsiatic), Indonesian (Austronesian) and Swedish (Indo-European). As a result, the \ufb01nal collection has\n17 languages from 7 distinct families that are displayed in Table 2.\nWe consider two approaches to investigate the relationship between dependency distance and word\nlength. First, a Kendall \u03c4 correlation test between the mean dependency distance score (D or \u2126) and\nmean word length (Ls or Lp). With respect to plain Pearson correlation, \u03c4 is more robust to extreme\nobservations and to non-linearity (Newson, 2002). A signi\ufb01cant negative correlation between mean \u2126\nand Ls or Lp would con\ufb01rm the prediction in Eq. 1. Note that \u2126and D have opposite interpretations\n(larger values of \u2126imply shorter dependencies whereas larger values of D imply longer dependencies)\nhence a positive correlation with respect to D would be analogous to a negative correlation with respect\nto \u2126. Therefore, a positive correlation between D and Ls or Lp could also be interpreted as con\ufb01rming\nthe prediction in Eq. 1 but D lacks the mathematical and statistical properties that are required for robust\nassessment (Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021).\nSecond, the fact that the Indo-European family is over-represented and the only one that is represented\nby more than one language (Table 2) motivates the need to control for the effect of family size. Ac-\ncordingly, we also consider a couple of kinds of generalized linear models. Fi<response clipped><NOTE>Due to the max output limit, only part of the full response has been shown to you.</NOTE>sent study with actual phonemic or syllabic lengths on PUD/PSUD goes beyond the\nscope of the present article. These considerations notwithstanding, notice that the stress of this article\nis on testing a prediction rather than theoretically agnostic data description. In this context, it is rather\nastonishing that the theoretical prediction (Eq. 1) is con\ufb01rmed even though the collections of sentences\nfor dependency distances and the collections for word lengths are independent. That con\ufb01rmation offers\ntwo major interpretations: (a) the results are due to biases in the sentences, either in PUD or in Fenk-\nOczlon\u2019s dataset (Fenk-Oczlon and Pilz, 2021) or, crucially, (b) there is actually a deep reason for the\nprediction to hold. Further research on a fully parallel scenario or alternative sources is required.\nBy having shown that DDm implies compression of word lengths, we do not mean that compression\nis produced exclusively by DDm. Our work does not rule out other sources for compression. Following\nprevious research testing successfully the prediction that DDm weakens in small sequences (Ferrer-i-\nCancho and G\u00b4omez-Rodr\u00b4\u0131guez, 2021; Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2021), we formulate a new prediction,\nnamely that compression per se (independently from DDm) may surface in short sequences. Future\nresearch should clarify the weight of the contribution to compression from DDm, compression itself and\nother principles.\nOnce one integrates our \ufb01ndings into the piece of a mathematical theory of communication in Table 1,\nit turns out that we have actually uncovered the following chain,\nDm \u2212\u2192DDm \u2212\u2192RUWm \u2212\u2192\nZipf\u2019s law of abbreviation\nNamely, a general principle of minimization of the distance between elements leads to the prediction of\nthe law of abbreviation.\nOur \ufb01ndings have implications for competing views and frameworks. First, the compensation hypoth-\nesis outlined in the introduction, i.e. less optimized languages at the level of dependency distances would\n\nhave more pressure for shorter words (or more optimized languages at the level of dependency distances\nwould tolerate longer words), would predict that the correlation between \u2126and word length should not\nbe negative. Instead, a zero or positive correlation would be expected depending on the strength or the\nnature of the compensation effect. Our \ufb01ndings of a negative correlation rule out the compensation hy-\npothesis as a primary explanation for the global trend. However, we cannot exclude that compensation\nhas some secondary role in general, an important role in speci\ufb01c languages or an important role with\ncertain domains of a language, as suggested for the suboptimal placement of clitics with respect to DDm\nin Romance languages (Ferrer-i-Cancho, 2015b). Second, DDm predicts compression and compression\nin turn predicts reduction (see Section 3.4 of Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017a)). By reduction, here we mean\nthe shortening or omission of predictable utterances, a phenomenon that has been used to justify the\nuniform information density and related hypotheses (see Lemke et al. (2021) and references therein).\nTherefore, the need for uniform information density and related hypotheses as standalone hypotheses\nneeds to be revised. Third, our \ufb01nding of an association between word length and dependency distance\nalso challenges the recent conclusion that (compositional) morphology and graphotactics can suf\ufb01ciently\naccount for most of the complexity of natural codes \u2013 as measured by code length (Pimentel et al., 2021)\nas if no strong independent pressure for compression (beyond morphology and graphotactics) really\nexisted. Our \ufb01ndings suggest that the two components of the problem of compression as de\ufb01ned in stan-\ndard information theory, i.e. the minimization of word length and the conditions of the coding scheme\n(Ferrer-i-Cancho et al., 2019), may not be as easy to dissociate from (compositional) morphology and\ngraphotactics as expected from the traditional reductionistic division into linguistic levels. Indeed, our\narticle demonstrates how constraints on the \u201csyntactic level\u201d (DDm) may be shaping the \u201c(sub)lexical\nlevel\u201d (compression on word lengths).\nOur work has implications beyond the current state of development of the theory of the communica-\ntion ef\ufb01ciency reviewed in Table 1. We have con\ufb01rmed that DDm implies compression but the outcome\nof our correlation analysis does not exclude that it could also the other way around, namely that com-\npression is actually leading to DDm. A possible track could be that pressure for shorter words may\nlead to a loss of information that would require more words to convey the same message, which in turn\nwould imply longer sentences and then higher pressure for DDm. Another track could be that a general\nprinciple of compression operates on top both at the level of words and at higher levels (phrases, clauses,\nsentences) and then close packaging (DDm) at all these levels is simply a consequence of maximizing\ncompression, in line with the now-or-never bottleneck (Christiansen and Chater, 2016). The author provided prompts and editorial oversight but did not originate the research idea, write code, or draft text. See the Ethics Statement and AI Disclosure section for full disclosure. All code and data are available at <https://github.com/typometrics/UDW26-Dependency-Length-Minimization> under a CC BY 4.0 license.\n\n###### Abstract\n\nDependency length minimization (DLM) is a well-documented processing universal, but previous studies report a single mean dependency distance (MDD) per language, obscuring variation across syntactic relation types. We analyze 122 languages in UD and SUD (version 2.17), showing that DLM operates on two distinct levels. Grammar-driven optimization targets functional dependencies (det, case, aux), which are universally short (mean 1.71, \u03c3\\sigma = 0.33) and invariant across typologically diverse languages. Processing-driven optimization operates on lexical dependencies (nsubj, obj, obl), which are longer (mean 2.87), highly variable (\u03c3\\sigma = 0.63), and constrained by word-order typology. This asymmetry holds in SUD despite reversed head direction (rr = 0.92). We conclude that \u201cthe grammar does the work\u201d of minimization by scaffolding sentences with local functional attachments, leaving processing pressures to determine the ordering of lexical heads.\n\nKeywords: dependency length minimization, Universal Dependencies, Surface-Syntactic UD, functional dependencies, lexical dependencies, syntactic typology\n\nKim Gerdes\n---\nUniversit\u00e9 Paris-Saclay, LISN (CNRS)\nOrsay, France\ngerdes@lisn.fr\n\nAbstract content\n\n## 1\\. Introduction\n\nThe tendency to minimize the linear distance between syntactically related words \u2014 dependency length minimization (DLM) \u2014 is one of the best-supported universals in quantitative linguistics (Futrell et al., 2015; Temperley and Gildea, 2018). Within dependency grammar, Hudson (1995) was the first to link dependency distance with processing difficulty. Gibson (1998) formalized this insight, proposing that sentence processing difficulty increases with the distance between a word and the head to which it must be integrated; minimizing dependency length thus reduces working memory demands during incremental parsing. Liu (2008) provided the first large-scale quantitative test of the dependency distance minimization hypothesis across languages and proposed mean dependency distance (MDD) as a metric of language comprehension difficulty. This cognitive motivation has been supported by extensive cross-linguistic evidence showing that observed dependency lengths are significantly shorter than random baselines (Gildea and Temperley, 2010; Futrell et al., 2020).\n\nDespite the robustness of the aggregate DLM signal, a fundamental question remains: _does DLM operate uniformly across all types of syntactic dependencies?_ Previous large-scale studies report a single MDD per language, aggregating dependencies as diverse as determiners (which must be adjacent to their noun) and subjects (which can be arbitrarily far from their verb). As Liu et al. (2022) noted in a diachronic study, \u201cdependency distance minimization is not universal across all dependency types,\u201d with only a subset of relation types responsible for the observed minimization effect.\n\nWe propose that DLM is not a uniform pressure, but operates on two distinct levels, corresponding to the fundamental distinction between _functional_ and _lexical_ projections in syntactic theory (Tesni\u00e8re, 1959; Mel\u2019\u010duk, 1988).\n\n  1. 1.\n\nGrammar-driven minimization: Functional heads (determiners, case markers, auxiliaries) are closed-class items whose position is strictly constrained by grammatical linearization rules. These rules \u201chard-code\u201d minimization by mandating adjacency (e.g., Det adjacent to Noun).\n\n  2. 2.\n\nProcessing-driven minimization: Lexical dependencies (subjects, objects, modifiers) involve open-class elements whose ordering is more flexible. Here, minimization is a soft constraint competing with information structure and other communicative needs.\n\n\n\n\nWe test this hypothesis on 122 languages (all UD/SUD v2.17 languages with \u2265\\geq500 sentences; see \u00a73.1) in both UD and SUD. We concatenate all treebanks per language to create a representative sample. This dual-framework comparison is methodologically important: Osborne and Gerdes (2019) showed that UD\u2019s content-word-headed convention inflates MDD, as function words are treated as dependents of distant lexical heads rather than as local heads themselves; converting to syntactic structures where function words head their phrases significantly reduces MDD. By contrasting UD and SUD, we disentangle annotation effects from processing patterns.\n\n## 2\\. Related Work\n\n### 2.1. Dependency Length Minimization\n\nDLM has a rich empirical history. Liu (2008) proposed MDD as a metric of language comprehension difficulty and was the first to test the DLM hypothesis quantitatively across languages; we note that MDD (the mean of per-dependency distances) differs from the dependency length (DL) sum used by Futrell et al. (2015) (see Niu and Liu, 2025, for a detailed discussion). Temperley (2008) identified three principles that minimize dependency length: consistent branching direction, shorter dependent phrases being closer to the head, and opposite-branching of one-word phrases. Gildea and Temperley (2010) confirmed that English dependency lengths are much closer to optimal than to random.\n\nFutrell et al. (2015) scaled this to 37 languages, demonstrating universal DLM, and Temperley and Gildea (2018) framed DLM as a \u201ctypological/cognitive universal\u201d. Ferrer-i Cancho et al. (2022) developed an optimality score framing word order as a spatial network optimization problem. More recently, Futrell et al. (2020) showed that dependency locality accurately predicts word-order preferences.\n\n### 2.2. Dependency Types and DLM\n\nMost critically for our work, a few studies have considered whether DLM varies across dependency types. Liu et al. (2022) examined diachronic changes in dependency distance by relation type in English, finding that only 9 types are responsible for overall minimization (including aux, mark, nsubj, and ccomp), while 6 types actually _increased_ in distance over time (including det, amod, and compound). Crucially, their study measures _diachronic trend direction_ \u2014 whether distances got shorter or longer across centuries \u2014 not absolute shortness. Their 9 minimizing types mix functional (aux, mark) and lexical (nsubj, ccomp) relations, because diachronic trends in English need not align with the synchronic functional/lexical distinction that holds _across languages_.\n\nDyer (2023) used a parallel corpus of 35 languages to revisit DLM, finding a \u201cmarkedly lesser extent\u201d of minimization in verb-final languages \u2014 an asymmetry we replicate and attribute to the lexical dependency component (\u00a74.3): verb-final languages display higher lexical MDD while functional MDD remains uniformly low. Gao and He (2024) used per-relation dependency distances to study syntactic complexity in Alzheimer\u2019s disease, finding that specific relation types like adverbial modifiers show differential patterns. Krielke (2024) showed that both scientific English and German increasingly utilize short, intra-phrasal dependency relations while long dependencies (clausal embeddings) become less favored over time \u2014 hinting at a functional/lexical split, though not explicitly framed as such.\n\nHowever, no previous study has systematically classified dependencies into functional and lexical categories and compared their DLM behavior at scale. Our contribution is the theoretically motivated, _a priori_ classification of relations into functional and lexical types, applied _synchronically_ across 122 languages, showing that the absolute distance gap between these categories is universal, not a language-specific historical trend.\n\n### 2.3. UD, SUD, and the Status of Function Words\n\nThe treatment of function words is central to our analysis. Universal Dependencies (de Marneffe et al., 2021) adopts a content-word-headed approach where function words (determiners, auxiliaries, adpositions) are dependents of lexical heads (Nivre, 2016). Osborne and Gerdes (2019) critiqued this convention, showing that UD\u2019s subordination of function words produces inflated MDD values compared to more syntactically motivated structures. They reported that MDD was \u201csignificantly reduced for nearly all languages\u201d when converting from UD to purely syntactic structures.\n\nSurface-Syntactic UD (Gerdes et al., 2018; Gerdes et al., 2021, SUD;) addresses this by promoting function words to head status where distributionally motivated: auxiliaries govern their verbs, adpositions govern their complements, complementizers govern their clauses. This reversal provides a natural test of robustness: since |p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(h\u200be\u200ba\u200bd)\u2212p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(d\u200be\u200bp)||pos(head)-pos(dep)| is symmetric, the same word pair produces the same distance regardless of which element is labeled head. If the functional\u2013lexical asymmetry is real, it must hold across both annotation conventions.\n\n### 2.4. Cognitive Models and DLM\n\nThe cognitive basis of DLM is rooted in memory constraints. Gibson (1998) proposed that both storage cost (keeping incomplete dependencies in memory) and integration cost (connecting incoming words to their heads) increase with dependency distance. Collins (2014) showed that DLM is complementary to information density optimization, suggesting that multiple cognitive pressures simultaneously shape word order. Stempniak (2024) further explored how DLM interacts with specific syntactic structures (coordination) in head-final languages, finding that dependency structure choices are driven by length minimization considerations. Our two-level model aligns with this: functional attachment has negligible integration cost (always local), while lexical attachment is the primary driver of processing difficulty.\n\n## 3\\. Data and Methodology\n\n### 3.1. Treebank Selection\n\nWe analyze all treebanks from UD v2.17 (Zeman et al., 2025). To ensure validity, we aggregate data at the language level: for each language, we concatenate all treebanks into a single corpus. We exclude languages with fewer than 500 sentences.11 1 A bootstrap stability analysis confirms that the functional\u2013lexical gap is robust from as few as 100 sentences (see \u00a74.6). This threshold excludes 64 low-resource languages (e.g., Guarani, Manx, Sanskrit), leaving 122 languages. This yields a matched set of 122 languages in UD and SUD, encompassing over 25 language families with major representation from Indo-European, Uralic, Afro-Asiatic, Tupian, Turkic, and Sino-Tibetan. For computational efficiency on very large languages, we cap the analysis at 15,000 sentences per language, which provides ample data for statistical stability. After filtering and capping, the UD dataset comprises 798,381 sentences and 11.2M non-punctuation dependency tokens across 122 languages (median 3,444 sentences per language; range 502\u201315,000). Of these tokens, 33% are functional dependencies and 67% are lexical, though the proportion varies considerably across languages (4%\u201349% functional), reflecting differences in morphological synthesis and the prevalence of function words.\n\n### 3.2. Functional vs. Lexical Classification\n\nFollowing the UD distinction between function words and content words (Nivre, 2016; de Marneffe et al., 2021), we classify dependency relations into two groups:\n\n  * \u2022\n\nFunctional: det, case, aux, mark, cop, cc, clf (and subtypes). These are closed-class dependencies that mark grammatical function.\n\n  * \u2022\n\nLexical: nsubj, obj, iobj, obl, nmod, amod, advmod22 2 Adverbs show mixed functional/lexical behavior. We follow UD in classifying advmod as lexical. A sensitivity analysis reclassifying it as functional actually strengthens the functional\u2013lexical distinction, confirming the result\u2019s robustness (see \u00a74.6)., advcl, acl, xcomp, ccomp, conj, compound, appos, flat, parataxis, etc.\n\n\n\n\nSUD adaptation. In SUD, function words become heads (Gerdes et al., 2018; Osborne and Gerdes, 2019), so the label inventory changes (see Table 2 in Appendix A). The key disambiguation concerns comp:obj and comp:obl: when the head is an adposition (UPOS = ADP) or complementizer (UPOS = SCONJ), the dependency is classified as _functional_ ; when the head is a verb, it is classified as _lexical_. Of 340k comp:* tokens across all SUD treebanks, 62.5% are classified functional and 37.5% lexical. Relations typically analyzed as non-dependency structure (e.g., punct, root) and underspecified relations (dep, orphan) are excluded.\n\n### 3.3. Metrics\n\nFor each group (overall, functional, lexical), we compute:\n\n  1. 1.\n\nMDD: Mean absolute distance |p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(h\u200be\u200ba\u200bd)\u2212p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(d\u200be\u200bp)||pos(head)-pos(dep)| over non-punctuation tokens, excluding root dependencies (Liu et al., 2017).\n\n  2. 2.\n\nRandom baseline: To estimate the expected distance under no DLM pressure, we randomly permute the _linear positions_ of all non-punctuation tokens in a sentence while keeping the dependency tree structure (i.e., who depends on whom) fixed. Each permutation reassigns every token to a new position, so the same tree is linearized in a different random order; dependency distances are then recomputed on this shuffled linearization. Following Futrell et al. (2015), we generate 20 such random permutations per sentence and average the resulting MDD across all permutations (MDDrand\\text{MDD}_{\\text{rand}}). This serves as a null hypothesis: the expected distance if word order carried no DLM signal.\n\n  3. 3.\n\nOptimality ratio (OR): MDDobs/MDDrand\\text{MDD}_{\\text{obs}}/\\text{MDD}_{\\text{rand}}, following the formalization of Ferrer-i Cancho et al. (2022). This provides a normalized measure of optimization: an OR of 1.0 suggests a language is no more optimized than chance, while values approaching 0.0 indicate extreme minimization.\n\n  4. 4.\n\nHead Directionality: The proportion of dependencies where the head follows the dependent (p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(h\u200be\u200ba\u200bd)>p\u200bo\u200bs\u200b(d\u200be\u200bp\u200be\u200bn\u200bd\u200be\u200bn\u200bt)pos(head)>pos(dependent)).\n\n\n\n\n## 4\\. Results\n\nFigure 1: Distribution of Functional vs. Lexical MDD across major language families. Functional MDD is consistently low across diverse families, whereas Lexical MDD varies significantly with word order typology (e.g., higher in head-final Turkic/Uralic/Dravidian, lower in head-initial Austronesian/Niger-Congo). Figure 2: (a) Distribution of functional (green) and lexical (red) MDD across 122 UD languages. Functional MDD clusters tightly around 1.71; lexical MDD is higher and more dispersed. (b) Boxplot comparison across UD and SUD frameworks: the functional\u2013lexical gap is preserved regardless of annotation convention.\n\n### 4.1. Overall DLM Confirmation\n\nAll 122 UD languages exhibit strong DLM. Observed MDD ranges from 1.44 to 3.67, far below random baselines (optimality ratios 0.17\u20130.89, mean 0.41). This confirms Futrell et al. (2015) at scale and extends the finding to new languages.\n\n### 4.2. The Functional\u2013Lexical Asymmetry\n\nFigure 3: (a) Functional vs. lexical MDD per language (122 UD languages). Every language is above the diagonal (y=xy=x).\n\nFigure 4: (b) Optimality ratio distributions for functional and lexical dependencies across 122 UD languages. Lower = more optimized.\n\n| Functional | Lexical\n---|---|---\nFramework | MDD | OR | MDD | OR\nUD (122) | 1.71\u00b1\\pm0.33 | 0.28 | 2.87\u00b1\\pm0.63 | 0.46\nSUD (122) | 1.65\u00b1\\pm0.32 | 0.27 | 2.48\u00b1\\pm0.47 | 0.41\nTable 1: Mean (\u00b1\\pm std) functional and lexical MDD across 122 qualifying languages per framework. Functional MDD is universally lower and less variable.\n\nTable 1 presents the central result, showing both absolute MDD and the optimality ratios derived from the 20-permutation random baselines. Three patterns emerge:\n\n1\\. Functional MDD is universally low. Across 122 UD languages, functional MDD averages 1.71 with a standard deviation of only 0.33. This narrow distribution (Figure 2) shows that grammars universally constrain function words to appear adjacent to their hosts, regardless of language family or word-order type.\n\n2\\. Lexical dependencies also show strong DLM. Crucially, lexical dependencies are not merely \u201cless optimized than functional\u201d: with a mean optimality ratio of 0.46 (\u03c3\\sigma = 0.15), they are 54% shorter than random baselines in every single language (122/122, OR range 0.20\u20130.93). This confirms that genuine processing-driven minimization operates on lexical dependencies \u2014 subjects, objects, and modifiers are placed substantially closer to their heads than chance would predict. However, lexical MDD is more variable (\u03c3\\sigma = 0.63 vs. 0.33), and SOV and V2 languages show higher values, consistent with Dyer (2023). Figure 4 confirms that lexical MDD universally exceeds functional MDD.\n\n3\\. The two levels differ in optimization depth. The mean functional optimality ratio is 0.28, versus 0.46 for lexical \u2014 both well below chance, but functional OR is 39% lower. This gap reflects different optimization mechanisms: functional adjacency is categorically enforced by grammar, while lexical ordering is a softer, gradient optimization that competes with information structure, heaviness, and other communicative pressures. The boxplot in Figure 2b confirms the pattern holds across both UD and SUD.\n\nStatistical confirmation. We verify the functional\u2013lexical gap with three tests, each addressing a different concern. _Is the gap consistent across languages?_ A paired Wilcoxon signed-rank test compares functional and lexical MDD within each language and asks whether one is systematically lower. The result (W=0W=0, p<10\u221218p<10^{-18}) confirms that functional MDD is lower in every single language, not just on average. The effect is large: Cohen\u2019s d=2.30d=2.30, meaning the gap exceeds two standard deviations. _Could the gap be driven by shared genealogical history?_ Related languages may share similar MDD patterns, inflating apparent universality. We analyze 122 languages in UD and SUD (version 2.17), showing that DLM operates on two distinct levels. Grammar-driven optimization targets functional dependencies (det, case, aux), which are universally short (mean 1.71, $\\sigma$ = 0.33) and invariant across typologically diverse languages. Processing-driven optimization operates on lexical dependencies (nsubj, obj, obl), which are longer (mean 2.87), highly variable ($\\sigma$ = 0.63), and constrained by word-order typology. This asymmetry holds in SUD despite reversed head direction (r = 0.92). We conclude that ''the grammar does the work'' of minimization by scaffolding sentences with local functional attachments, leaving processing pressures to determine the ordering of lexical heads.\n\nSubjects: |  Computation and Language (cs.CL)\n---|---\nCite as: | [arXiv:2607.01899](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01899) [cs.CL]\n  | (or {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04223\nType: HTML\nLength: 7162 chars\n\n--- Content ---\n\nSkip to main content [](https://arxiv.org/IgnoreMe) [ ](https://arxiv.org/) [ Search ](https://arxiv.org/search) [Submit](https://arxiv.org/user/create) [Donate](https://info.arxiv.org/about/donate.html) [Log in](https://arxiv.org/login)\n\nSearch arXiv\n\nPress Enter to search * [Advanced search](https://arxiv.org/search/advanced)\n\n# Computer Science > Computation and Language\n\n**arXiv:2609.04223** (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 6 Jul 2026]\n\n# Title:How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?\n\nAuthors:[Sirui Chen](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Chen,+S)\n\nView a PDF of the paper titled How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?, by Sirui Chen\n\n[View PDF](/pdf/2609.04223) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.04223v1)\n\n> Abstract:Dependency-distance estimates derived from a single corpus are routinely treated as properties of a language, yet this assumption has not been tested across independently compiled corpora. We compared mean dependency-distance estimates across 38 same-language treebank pairs from Universal Dependencies v2.18, using concordance correlation, Bland-Altman analysis, and a twelve-specification multiverse design. Cross-treebank agreement was moderate at best: substituting one treebank for another reversed nearly 40 percent of pairwise language orderings, and treebank choice accounted for roughly 29 percent of between-group variance. This disagreement substantially exceeded within-treebank sampling error and persisted across all twelve preprocessing specifications. Nevertheless, every treebank confirmed dependency-length minimization (normalized ratio below 1). The data are more consistent with MDD as a corpus-conditioned composite of grammatical, register, and annotation factors than as a stable language-level parameter: the qualitative DLM universal survives corpus substitution, but the ordinal cross-linguistic ranking does not.\n\nComments: | 18 pages, 3 figures. We compared mean dependency-distance estimates across 38 same-language treebank pairs from Universal Dependencies v2.18, using concordance correlation, Bland-Altman analysis, and a twelve-specification multiverse design. Cross-treebank agreement was moderate at best: substituting one treebank for another reversed nearly 40 percent of pairwise language orderings, and treebank choice accounted for roughly 29 percent of between-group variance. This disagreement substantially exceeded within-treebank sampling error and persisted across all twelve preprocessing specifications. Nevertheless, every treebank confirmed dependency-length minimization (normalized ratio below 1). The data are more consistent with MDD as a corpus-conditioned composite of grammatical, register, and annotation factors than as a stable language-level parameter: the qualitative DLM universal survives corpus substitution, but the ordinal cross-linguistic ranking does not.\n\nComments: | 18 pages, 3 figures. ### [111] THINKING · 2026-09-08 00:16:23 UTC

```
Good, now I have the key details from FC&GR 2021. Let me continue gathering information on other papers and then synthesize.

Key findings from FC&GR 2021:
- Sample: 17 languages from 7 families (PUD v2.6)
- Metric: Ω (normalized score) and D (sum of dependency distances)
- Word length measured in phonemes (Lp) and syllables (Ls)
- Word length data from Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) - borrowed from another study
- Found: Ω and Lp negatively correlated (τ = -0.37, p = 0.039) - confirms DDm predicts compression in phonemes


- No significant correlation between Ω and Ls (syllables), but D and Lp also show negative correlation (τ = -0.459, p = 0.011)
- The data isn't fully parallel since word lengths come from a different source than the dependency distances, though this actually matters for my study because I'm testing the reverse direction—whether phonological density predicts DLM strength
- My null finding (phonological density doesn't predict DLM) complements their positive finding rather than contradicts it, suggesting the relationship is directional
- I still need to pull in details from Gerdes 2026, check the Dobrovoljc attribution for the Chen paper, and gather information on Futrell 2015, Futrell 2020, Coupé 2019, and Krielke 2024
```

universal, but it has few indisputably universal characteristics, with cross-linguistic variation being the norm. For example, languages differ greatly in the number of syllables they allow, resulting in large variation in the Shannon information per syllable. Nevertheless, all natural languages allow their speakers to efficiently encode and transmit information. We show here, using quantitative methods on a large cross-linguistic corpus of 17 languages, that the coupling between language-level (information per syllable) and speaker-level (speech rate) properties results in languages encoding similar information rates (~39 bits/s) despite wide differences in each property individually: Languages are more similar in information rates than in Shannon information or speech rate. These findings highlight the intimate feedback loops between languages\u2019 structural properties and their speakers\u2019 neurocognition and biology under communicative pressures. Thus, language is the product of a multiscale communicative niche construction process at the intersection of biology, environment, and culture.\n\n[ ](https://www.researchgate.net/figure/SR-and-IR-The-distribution-of-SR-in-syllables-per-second-left-and-IR-in-bits-per_fig1_335633446 \"Fig. 1. SR and IR. The distribution of SR \\(in syllables per second\\)...\")\n\n[SR and IR. The distribution of SR (in syllables per second) (left) and IR (in bits per second) (right) within the languages in our database (colored areas; colors represent the language families) and across them (black areas at the top) using a Gaussian kernel density estimate. The black vertical lines spanning the whole plot represent the means (solid lines) \u00b1 1 SD (dashed lines). The short black vertical lines represent the actual data points.\u2026 ](https://www.researchgate.net/figure/SR-and-IR-The-distribution-of-SR-in-syllables-per-second-left-and-IR-in-bits-per_fig1_335633446)\n\n[ ](https://www.researchgate.net/figure/Relationship-between-SR-and-ID-across-languages-Colors-represent-the-language-families_fig2_335633446 \"Fig. 2. Distributed\n\nunder a Creative\n\nCommons Attribution\n\nNonCommercial\n\nLicense 4.0 (CC BY-NC).\n\nDifferent languages, similar encoding efficiency:\n\nComparable information rates across the human\n\ncommunicative niche\n\nChristophe Coup\u00e9\n\n1,2\n\n*, Yoon Oh\n\n3,4\n\n*, Dan Dediu\n\n1,5\n\n, Fran\u00e7ois Pellegrino\n\n1\u2020\n\nLanguage is universal, but it has few indisputably universal characteristics, with cross-linguistic variation being the\n\nnorm. For example, languages differ greatly in the number of syllables they allow, resulting in large variation in the\n\nShannon information per syllable. Nevertheless, all natural languages allow theirspeakers to efficiently encodeand\n\ntransmit information. We show here, using quantitative methods on a large cross-linguistic corpus of 17 languages,\n\nthat the coupling between language-level (information per syllable) and speaker-level (speech rate) properties\n\nresults in languages encoding similar information rates (~39 bits/s) despite wide differences in each property indi-\n\nvidually: Languages are more similar in information rates than in Shannon information or speech rate. These find-\n\nings highlight the intimate feedback loops between languages\u2019structural properties and their speakers\u2019\n\nneurocognition and biology under communicative pressures. Thus, language is the product of a multiscale com-\n\nmunicative niche construction process at the intersection of biology, environment, and culture.\n\nINTRODUCTION\n\nLanguage is universally used by all human groups, but it hardly dis-\n\nplays undisputable universal characteristics, with a few possible excep-\n\ntions related to pragmatic and communicative constraints (1,2). This\n\nubiquity comes with very high levels ofvariation across the 7000 or so\n\nlanguages (3). For example, linguistic differences between Japanese\n\nand English lead to a ratio of 1:11 in their number of distinct syllables.\n\nThese differences in repertoire size result in large variation in the\n\namount of information they encode per syllable according to Shannon\u2019s\n\ntheory of communication. Despite those differences, Japanese and\n\nEnglish endow their respective speakers with linguistic systems that\n\nfulfill equally well one of the most important roles of spoken commu-\n\nnication, namely, information transmission. We show here that the\n\ninterplay between language-specific structural properties (as reflected\n\nby the amount of information per syllable) and speaker-level language\n\nprocessingand production [as reflected by speech rate (SR)] leads lan-\n\nguages to gravitate around an information rate (IR) of about 39 bits/s.\n\nThis finding, based on quantitative methods applied to a large cross-\n\nlinguistic corpus of 17 languages, highlights the intimate feedback loops\n\nbetween languages and their speakers due to communicative pressures.\n\nWe suggest that this phenomenon is rooted in the human neurocogni-\n\ntive capacity, probably present in our lineage for a long time (4), and\n\nthat human language can be analyzed as the product of a multiscale\n\ncommunicative and cultural niche construction process involving bi-\n\nology, environment, and culture (5).\n\nEach human language provides its speakers with a communication\n\nsystem that fulfills their needs for transmitting information to their\n\npeers. The Uniform Information Density hypothesis (6)andsimilar\n\napproaches [e.g., (7)and(8)] suggested that speakers distribute\n\ninformation along the speech signal following a smooth distribution\n\nrather than high-amplitude fluctuations. Compatible with Shannon\u2019s\n\ntheory, this optimization process guarantees the robust information\n\ntransmission at a rate close to the channel capacity. We adopt here\n\na quite different perspective, where we compare, across very different\n\nlanguages, the average rates at which information is emitted. This ap-\n\nproach enables us to estimate the channel capacity and to assess whether\n\nthe large differences observed among languages in terms of encoding\n\nresult in analog differences in channel capacity or, conversely, whether\n\nthere exist compensating strategies that go beyond the local adaptation\n\noperating during speech production. Therefore, we investigate the inter-\n\naction between information encoding and average SR and, more specif-\n\nically, whether the variation among languages in IR is regulated by\n\ncommunicative constraints. Thus, does too low an IR hinder commu-\n\nnicative efficiency? And, at the other extreme, does pushing it too high\n\nincur too heavy physiological and cognitive costs? While a negative\n\ncorrelation between average SR and the informativeness of linguistic\n\nconstituents has been demonstrated in a small multilanguage corpus\n\n(9), the distribution of IRs across human languages is almost totally\n\nunknown despite its crucial importance for understanding human\n\nspoken communication. While our data here come only from speech\n\nproduction (information encoding), our results, nevertheless, implic-\n\nitly address also speech perception (information retrieval) and proces-\n\nsing, as they are all intimately coupled and coevolve during language\n\nacquisition, use, and change (10).\n\nWe have chosen to focus here on the syllable as the information-\n\nencoding unit for both linguistic and cognitive reasons. On the linguistic\n\nside, despite a long-lasting debate in phonology about whether the syl-\n\nlable is a universal unit in the world\u2019s languages (11) being a corner-\n\nstone of this controversy, analyzing the encoding of information in\n\nterms of syllables does offer several advantages over other levels of lin-\n\nguistic description (such as phonemes and morphemes). First, syllables\n\nare much less prone than phonemes to complete deletion in casual\n\nspeech, allowing more robust estimates of SR (12) [readers can also refer\n\nto (9) for a more detailed discussion on this matter]. Moreover, we chose\n\nthesyllableovermeaning-bearingunits(morphemesorwords),asthe\n\nlatter levels rely more on a language-specific linguistic analysis, on top of\n\nvarious methodological difficulties affecting their robust counting in a\n\ncross-linguistic framework (see text S1 and fig. S1 for a discussion on the\n\n1\n\nLaboratoireDynamique du Langage UMR5596, CNRS,University of Lyon, 14 Avenue\n\nBerthelot, 69007 Lyon, France.\n\n2\n\nDepartment of Linguistics, The University of\n\nHong Kong, Pokfulam Road, Hong Kong.\n\n3\n\nNew Zealand Institute of Language, Brain\n\nand Behaviour, University of Canterbury, Private Bag 4800, Christchurch 8140,\n\nNew Zealand.\n\n4\n\nDepartment of French Language and Literature, Ajou University,\n\nSuwon, 16499 Gyeonggi-do, South Korea.\n\n5\n\nCollegium de Lyon, University of Lyon, 24\n\nrue Baldassini, 69007 Lyon, France.\n\n*These authors contributed equally to this work.\n\n\u2020Corresponding author.
