{"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2109.08900\nType: HTML\nLength: 44661 chars (truncated)\n\n--- Content ---\n\n# Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\n\nRamon Ferrer-i-Cancho  Affiliation: Complexity and Quantitative Linguistics Lab,  Affiliation: LARCA Research Group  Affiliation: Departament de Ci\u00e8ncies de la Computaci\u00f3  Affiliation: Universitat Polit\u00e8cnica de Catalunya  Affiliation: Campus Nord, Edifici Omega  Affiliation: Jordi Girona Salgado 1-3  Affiliation: 08034 Barcelona, Catalonia, Spain  Email: [rferrericancho@cs.upc.edu](mailto:) Carlos G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez  Affiliation: Universidade da Coru\u00f1a, CITIC  Affiliation: FASTPARSE Lab, LyS Research Group  Affiliation: Departamento de Ciencias  Affiliation: de la Computaci\u00f3n y Tecnolog\u00edas  Affiliation: de la Informaci\u00f3n  Affiliation: Facultade de Inform\u00e1tica, Elvi\u00f1a,  Affiliation: 15071, A Coru\u00f1a, Spain  Email: [cgomezr@udc.es](mailto:)\n\n###### Abstract\n\nDependency distance minimization (DDm) is a well-established principle of word order. It has been predicted theoretically that DDm implies compression, namely the minimization of word lengths. This is a second order prediction because it links a principle with another principle, rather than a principle and a manifestation as in a first order prediction. Here we test that second order prediction with a parallel collection of treebanks controlling for annotation style with Universal Dependencies and Surface-Syntactic Universal Dependencies. To test it, we use a recently introduced score that has many mathematical and statistical advantages with respect to the widely used sum of dependency distances. We find that the prediction is confirmed by the new score when word lengths are measured in phonemes, independently of the annotation style, but not when word lengths are measured in syllables. In contrast, one of the most widely used scores, i.e. the sum of dependency distances, fails to confirm that prediction, showing the weakness of raw dependency distances for research on word order. Finally, our findings expand the theory of natural communication by linking two distinct levels of organization, namely syntax (word order) and word internal structure.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nAccording to the dependency distance minimization (DDm) principle, the distance between heads and their dependent words in a sentence has to be reduced [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2004, Gildea and Temperley (2007]. The principle has been supported widely by studies whose coverage of languages and families is increasing over time (e.g., ?), ?), ?), ?)). For simplicity, such distance is usually measured in words (see ?) for an exception) but it could be measured with more precision in syllables or phonemes [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b]. In that way, the distance of a dependency would be a function of the length of the words defining the dependency and that of the words in-between. For this reason, it was predicted that word lengths should be minimized to minimize dependency distances [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017a, Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017b], namely the DDm principle predicts compression, i.e., another principle whereby LL, the mean word length, has to be minimized. However, to our knowledge, such a prediction has never been tested in spite of its great theoretical importance. First, it is crucial for the construction of a theory of language and other natural communication systems [Semple et al. (2021]. A critical component of a theory are the predictions that it can make. For instance, DDm predicts the scarcity of crossing dependencies [G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez and Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017] and compression of word lengths predicts Zipf\u2019s law of abbreviation [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2019]. These are examples of first order predictions, namely manifestations that are predicted by a certain principle. The focus of this article are second order predictions, namely, principles that are predicted by other principles, such as (a) the prediction of DDm from Dm, a general principle of distance minimization [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021, Ferrer-i-Cancho (2003] in the spirit of Behaghel\u2019s pioneering views [Behaghel (1932], or (b) a principle of surprisal or entropy minimization (H\u200bmHm) from compression [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2018]. Table 1 summarizes these and other first order and second order predictions from previous research stemming from a general principle of energy minimization (Em) in the spirit of Zipf\u2019s least effort principle [Zipf (1949] but extended to other biological systems [Semple et al. (2021].\n\nIn the quantitative linguistics tradition, syllables are considered to be one of the best units (if not the best one) for measuring word length, mainly to warrant cross-linguistic validity [Popescu et al. (2013, Grzybek (2013]. In addition, phonemes (and graphemes) are considered to not be appropriate because they are not immediate constituents of words (words are made of syllables that are made in turn of phonemes). Here we will revise this view arising from research on word lengths and check if it still applies for research on the interaction between dependency distance (in words) and word length.\n\nThis article is aimed at testing the hypothesis that DDm implies compression, which we refer to as repertoire unit weight minimization (RUWm). We follow the convention that a lower case mm at the end of the abbreviation of a principle indicates \u201dminimization\u201d [Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021]. RUWm is the minimization of the weight (or cost) of units in a repertoire. In the context of word types and their length as their weight, RUWm corresponds to the minimization of word lengths. RUWm, predicts that more likely units in a repertoire (e.g., more frequent words in a vocabulary) should be lighter (e.g., be shorter). In particular, RUWm has been argued to lead to the emergence of the law of abbreviation across linguistic levels [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2019]: e.g., the lexical level where frequent word types or meanings tend to have shorter forms [Zipf (1949, Kanwal et al. (2017, Brochhagen (2021], and the sublexical level where more frequent cases or case marker types tend to have shorter forms [Liu (2021]. RUWm is a specialization of a more general principle of unit weight minimization (UWm) arising from research on unifying compression with the origins of both the law of abbreviation and Menzerath\u2019s law [Gustison et al. (2016]. The law of abbreviation is the tendency of more frequent words to be shorter [Zipf (1949] while Menzerath\u2019s law is the tendency of linguistic constructs with more parts to be made of smaller parts [Altmann (1980]. A specialization of UWm on sequences, sequence unit weight minimization (SUWm) sheds light on the possible origin of Menzerath\u2019s law (?); Table 1). In the context of words as sequences of syllables and the length of as syllable as its weight (or cost), SUWm corresponds to the minimization of the lengths of syllables in the words they appear. SUWm predicts that units in longer sequences (e.g., syllables in words with more syllables) should have smaller weight (e.g., be shorter).\n\nFormally, we aim to test\n\n| D\u200bD\u200bm\u27f6R\u200bU\u200bW\u200bm.DDm\\longrightarrow RUWm. |  | (1)\n---|---|---|---\n\nAs an alternative to that prediction one could consider a compensation hypothesis, where less optimized languages at the level of dependency distances would have more pressure for shorter words (or more optimized languages at the level of dependency distances would tolerate longer words). The idea of compensation has been applied in word order research in different ways [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2018, Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b]. For instance, the suboptimal placement of the verb in SOV orders with respect to DDm (DDm predicts SVO or OVS) has been argued to be compensated by the short length of clitics [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b].\n\nEm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | Dm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | SDm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | pairs of primarily alternating word orders [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2016]\n---|---|---|---|---|---|---\n|  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | DDm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | acceptability [Morrill (2000]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | word order preferences [Morrill (2000]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | scarcity of crossing dependencies\n|  |  |  |  |  | [G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez and Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | tendency to uncover the root [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2008]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | projectivity & planarity with high probability\n|  |  |  | DDm (+ projectivity) | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | medial placement of the root\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Gildea and Temperley (2007, Alemany-Puig et al. (2022]\n|  |  |  | DDm (+ planarity) | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | medial placement of the central vertex\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Iordanskii (1987, Hochberg and Stallmann (2003, Alemany-Puig et al. (2022]\n|  |  |  | DDm \\+ extreme V placement | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | placement of adjectives with respect to nominal heads\n|  |  |  | in SVO triples |  | [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2008, Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015a]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | placement of auxiliary V with respect to main V\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2008]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | consistent branching for dependents of nominal heads\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | \u201dunnecessity\u201d of headedness parameter\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2015b]\n|  |  |  |  | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | RUWm (to be tested in this article)\n| \u27f6\\longrightarrow | UWm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | RUWm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | Zipf\u2019s law of abbreviation\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Shannon (1948, Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2019]\n|  |  |  |  |  | reduction\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017a]\n|  |  |  | RUWm + | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | Hm [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2018]\n|  |  |  | unique segmentation |  |\n|  |  |  | SUWm | \u27f6\\longrightarrow | Menzerath\u2019s law\n|  |  |  |  |  | [Gustison et al. (2016, Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2019]\nTable 1:  Optimization principles and their predictions. Arrows link principles with their predictions. Predictions can take the form of principles or manifestations. Principles are marked in boldface. The two principles whose relationship is the target of this article are marked in blue. Em: energy minimization. Dm: distance minimization. UWm: unit weight minimization, popularly known as compression. RUWm: repertoire unit weight minimization. SUWm: sequence unit weight minimization. SDm: swap distance minimization. DDm: dependency distance minimization. Hm: entropy (or surprisal) minimization. We use parentheses for assumptions that are likely to be predictions of DDm and thus likely to be unnecessary assumptions to a large extent (see ?) for further details about the argument). Awareness of such unnecessary assumptions is vital for the construction of a parsimonious theory.\n\n## 2 Methods\n\nTo measure dependency distance in a sentence, we considered two scores, both computed using dependency distances measured in words. The first score is \u03a9\\Omega, a recently introduced normalized score that takes the value 11 when the dependency distances are fully optimized and is expected to take a value of 00 if there is no bias on dependency distances, for or against DDm [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. \u03a9\\Omega can be seen as score of the intensity of DDm, which is maximum when \u03a9=1\\Omega=1, and missing when \u03a9\u22480\\Omega\\approx 0. \u03a9<0\\Omega<0 indicates that DDm is surpassed by other word order principles. The score has the virtue of satisfying a series of mathematical and statistical properties: dual normalization (i.e. normalization with respect to both the minimum and the random baseline), constancy under minimum linear arrangement, stability under random linear arrangement, invariance under linear transformation and boundedness under maximum linear arrangement [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. These properties are particularly useful when calculating an average score over the sentences of a treebank. For comparison, we consider also DD, the sum of dependencies of a sentence. While \u03a9\\Omega is a measure of closeness, DD is a measure of distance. DD is the most widely used score [Gildea and Temperley (2007, Gildea and Temperley (2010, Futrell et al. (2015, Futrell et al. (2020] but does not satisfy any of the remarkable mathematical properties enumerated above. The intensity of DDm is difficult to assess just from the value of DD.\n\nFor each treebank considered in this study, we calculated an average \u03a9\\Omega and average DD over all the sentences of the treebank. To measure word length, we considered two different units: phonemes and syllables. For each language, we calculated LsL_{s}, the mean word length in syllables (mean syllables per word token) and LpL_{p}, the mean word length in phonemes (mean phonemes per word token).\n\nTo control for the content or the source text of the treebanks, we used a parallel collection of treebanks, in particular, the Parallel Universal Dependencies (PUD) collection version 2.6 [Zeman et al. (2017]. PUD contains 20 languages from 9 distinct families. PUD follows the UD annotation style [Zeman et al. (2020]. To control for annotation style, we also use SUD, i.e. Surface-Syntactic Universal Dependencies [Gerdes et al. (2018]. We use PSUD to refer to the PUD collection following the SUD annotation [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. The PUD and PSUD treebank collections are borrowed from a recent study [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. The preprocessing of these treebanks involves the removal of punctuation marks and reparalellization to warrant there is no loss of parallelism after punctuation mark removal [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. The data are available from <https://github.com/lluisalemanypuig/optimality-syntactic-dependency-distances> in two levels of preprocessing: (a) the preprocessed treebanks as head vectors and (b) the raw text tables that were extracted from them and used to feed the statistical analyses. The transformation of the raw head vectors into the raw text tables can be replicated easily with the Linear Arrangement Library [Alemany-Puig and Ferrer-i-Cancho (2022].\n\nThe PUD data does not include syllable or phoneme annotations that would allow us to measure word lengths in these units, and obtaining them would be highly costly. Thus, we instead borrowed mean word lengths from the dataset of another recent study [Fenk-Oczlon and Pilz (2021]. In that study, mean word lengths were estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences encoding one proposition and using basic vocabulary. Three languages from PUD/PSUD are missing in that dataset: Arabic (Afro-Asiatic), Indonesian (Austronesian) and Swedish (Indo-European). As a result, the final collection has 17 languages from 7 distinct families that are displayed in Table 2.\n\nWe consider two approaches to investigate the relationship between dependency distance and word length. First, a Kendall \u03c4\\tau correlation test between the mean dependency distance score (DD or \u03a9\\Omega) and mean word length (LsL_{s} or LpL_{p}). With respect to plain Pearson correlation, \u03c4\\tau is more robust to extreme observations and to non-linearity [Newson (2002]. A significant negative correlation between mean \u03a9\\Omega and LsL_{s} or LpL_{p} would confirm the prediction in Eq. 1. Note that \u03a9\\Omega and DD have opposite interpretations (larger values of \u03a9\\Omega imply shorter dependencies whereas larger values of DD imply longer dependencies) hence a positive correlation with respect to DD would be analogous to a negative correlation with respect to \u03a9\\Omega. Therefore, a positive correlation between DD and LsL_{s} or LpL_{p} could also be interpreted as confirming the prediction in Eq. 1 but DD lacks the mathematical and statistical properties that are required for robust assessment [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021].\n\nSecond, the fact that the Indo-European family is over-represented and the only one that is represented by more than one language (Table 2) motivates the need to control for the effect of family size. Accordingly, we also consider a couple of kinds of generalized linear models. First, a null model with mean word length as response (LsL_{s} or LpL_{p}) and language family as random factor. Second, a mixed effects model with mean word length as response (LsL_{s} or LpL_{p}), language family as random effect and a dependency distance score (mean DD or mean \u03a9\\Omega) as fixed effect. To test the prediction with these models, we use information theoretic model selection [Burnham and Anderson (2002, Winter (2019]. AIC of the mixed effects model being lower than that of the null model would confirm the prediction provided that the weight of the association between the predictor and the response has a sign that matches that of the prediction. Again, some caution is needed when DD is involved because of its technical limitations [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021].\n\nThe linear models were fitted with the lme4 R package. Confidence intervals for the weight of the fixed effect were computed using the parametric bootstrapping method of function confint of the lme4 package.\n\nFamily |  Languages\n---|---\nTurkic (1) |  Turkish\nIndo-European (11) |  Czech, English, French, German, Hindi, Icelandic, Italian, Polish, Portuguese, Russian, Spanish\nJaponic (1) |  Japanese\nKoreanic (1) |  Korean\nSino-Tibetan (1) |  Chinese\nTai-Kadai (1) |  Thai\nUralic (1) |  Finnish\nTable 2:  The 17 languages from 7 families used in the present study. (d) LpL_{p} as a function of mean \u03a9\\Omega in PSUD.  Figure 2:  Mean word length (LL) as a function of the mean sum of dependency distances (mean DD). (a) LsL_{s} as a function of DD in PUD. (b) LsL_{s} as a function of DD in PSUD. (c) LpL_{p} as a function of mean DD in PUD. (d) LpL_{p} as a function of mean DD in PSUD. We assume a significance level of 0.05.  Collection | distance | length | AIC mixed effects | AIC null\n---|---|---|---|---\nPUD | \u03a9\\Omega | LsL_{s} | 23.15 | 23.9\n|  | LpL_{p} | 42.16 | 47.64\nPSUD | \u03a9\\Omega | LsL_{s} | 23.54 | 23.9\n|  | LpL_{p} | 42.96 | 47.64\nPUD | DD | LsL_{s} | 32.28 | 23.9\n|  | LpL_{p} | 45.23 | 47.64\nPSUD | DD | LsL_{s} | 32.22 | 23.9\n|  | LpL_{p} | 48.03 | 47.64\nTable 4:  Information theoretic selection of models to predict the mean word length. Table 3 indicates that the correlation between \u03a9\\Omega and LpL_{p} is negative and statistically significant whereas the correlation between \u03a9\\Omega and LsL_{s} is also negative but not significant. That is, the shorter the syntactic dependencies of a language upon dual normalization (\u03a9\\Omega), the shorter the words when their length is measured in phonemes. Table 4 indicates that the result is confirmed by a linear mixed effects model that predicts LsL_{s} or LpL_{p} based on \u03a9\\Omega with family as random effect. The null model (only family as random factor) always yields an AIC value that is larger than that of the mixed effects model (\u03a9\\Omega as fixed effect and family as random effect). However, the AIC of the null model is only sufficiently large (i.e., differing by various units) when the predictor is LsL_{s}, in agreement with the plain correlation analysis (Table 3). The analysis of the confidence intervals supports the conclusions: the confidence interval for the weight of \u03a9\\Omega with LpL_{p} as response comprises exclusively negative values, consistently with a negative correlation between \u03a9\\Omega and LpL_{p} (Table 5).\n\nCollection | Fixed effect | Response | Lower | Upper\n---|---|---|---|---\nPUD | \u03a9\\Omega | LpL_{p} | -10.85 | -1.19\nPSUD | \u03a9\\Omega | LpL_{p} | -9.21 | -0.69\nPUD | DD | LsL_{s} | -0.03 | 0\n|  | LpL_{p} | -0.07 | -0.02\nPSUD | DD | LsL_{s} | -0.03 | 0.01\n|  | LpL_{p} | -0.07 | -0.02\nTable 5:  Lower and upper bounds of the 95%95\\% confidence interval for the weight of the fixed effect (\u03a9\\Omega or DD) in the mixed effects models. The confidence intervals for \u03a9\\Omega with LpL_{p} as response could not be computed due to numerical problems.\n\nIf \u03a9\\Omega is replaced by DD in the preceding analyses, opposite conclusions are drawn concerning the direction of the correlation. First, Figure 2 suggests that mean word length tends to decrease as DD increases when word length is measured in phonemes in both PUD and PSUD. Accordingly, Table 3 confirms that the correlation between DD and LpL_{p} is negative and statistically significant while the correlation between DD and LsL_{s} turns out to not be significant. That is, the longer the syntactic dependencies of a language, the shorter the words when their length is measured in phonemes, the opposite conclusion that is reached with \u03a9\\Omega. Table 4 indicates that the results are confirmed by linear mixed effects linear models that predict LsL_{s} or LpL_{p} based on DD with family as random effect. When measuring word lengths in syllables, the AIC of the null model is much smaller than that of the mixed effects model with DD as predictor, supporting that DD and LsL_{s} are uncorrelated. In contrast, the AIC of the null model is larger (by more than unit) than that of the mixed effects model with DD as predictor, supporting that DD and LpL_{p} are correlated. The analysis of the confidence intervals (Table 5) confirms a negative correlation between DD and LpL_{p} because the confidence intervals for the weight of DD with LpL_{p} as response comprise exclusively negative values; that does not happen when LsL_{s} is the response.\n\n## 4 Discussion\n\nWe have confirmed the prediction that DDm leads to compression (Eq. 1) by showing that \u03a9\\Omega and LpL_{p} are negatively correlated: the closer the syntactically related words, the shorter the words. Although syllables are the preferred unit of measurement of word length over phonemes in quantitative linguistics [Popescu et al. (2013, Grzybek (2013], we have failed to confirm the prediction with that unit. We cannot exclude the possibility that a negative correlation between \u03a9\\Omega and LsL_{s} exists but has not surfaced because it is weaker and the sample of languages is not large enough. However, the failure with syllables may be a confirmation of the higher capacity of phonemes over syllables to capture the so-called \u2019phonological complexity\u2019 [Pimentel et al. (2020]. Furthermore, we suspect that word lengths in phonemes lead eventually to a more accurate estimation of the true distance between words, currently measured in words, than syllables. To see the relationship between word length and dependency distance and to build an explanation, suppose that \u03b4p\\delta_{p} is the phonemic distance between two words, \u03b4s\\delta_{s} is their syllabic distance and dd is their distance in words (d=1d=1 if two words are consecutive). Then assuming that a word and its dependent word are connected by the middle of their phonemic or syllabic sequence, a mean field approximation yields \u03b4p\u2248d\u200bLp\\delta_{p}\\approx dL_{p} (the head and their dependent contribute with Lp/2L_{p}/2 phonemes; the words in-between the head and the governor contribute with (d\u22121)\u200bLp(d-1)L_{p} phonemes). Analogously, \u03b4s\u2248d\u200bLs\\delta_{s}\\approx dL_{s}. Then \u03b4p\\delta_{p} may be more strongly correlated with the time that is needed to keep unresolved dependencies in memory [Morrill (2000] than \u03b4s\\delta_{s} because syllables vary in phonemic length. That time was considered to be the key to understand what determines the acceptability of sentences and other word order phenomena [Morrill (2000]. Then \u03b4p\\delta_{p} looks like a better proxy for the combination of memory decay and interference that is believed to cause DDm [Liu et al. (2017, Temperley and Gildea (2018]. To recap, the argument that syllables are more appropriate units than phonemes (or graphemes) because syllables are immediate word constituents [Popescu et al. (2013, Grzybek (2013] may not be appropriate for research on dependency distances because distances (or time) could be measured more accurately with constituents located farther down in the hierarchy of constituents.\n\nWe have also shown that DD, a widely used dependency score [Gildea and Temperley (2007, Gildea and Temperley (2010, Futrell et al. (2015, Futrell et al. (2020], fails to confirm the prediction that DDm implies compression. It is not the first time that \u03a9\\Omega shows a superior performance when testing theoretical predictions. When testing that DDm should be surpassed by other word order principles in short sentences [Ferrer-i-Cancho (2014, Ferrer-i-Cancho (2017a], \u03a9\\Omega was able to find many more languages with anti-DDm effects than DD [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021]. Our findings reinforce the view that raw dependency distances are a poor reflect of DDm and that advanced scores such as \u03a9\\Omega are crucial for progress in research on that optimization principle and related memory constraints [Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021], and eventually, for the construction of a general theory of natural systems with human language as a particular case and energy minimization at the center [Semple et al. (2021]. An early sketch of that theory is shown in Table 1.\n\nFor each language, we have measured dependency distances (\u03a9\\Omega and DD) on the collection of sentences included in PUD/PSUD whereas mean word lengths (LpL_{p} and LsL_{s}) come from an independent collection of sentences [Fenk-Oczlon and Pilz (2021]. It could be argued that mean word lengths should have been estimated on PUD/PSUD, too. This would make the whole study fully parallel, and word length data potentially more accurate, as it would come from a larger sample and, more specifically, from the same sentences where dependency lengths have been measured. It is conceivable, for example, that a given kind of syntactic construction could produce shorter dependency distances in language A than in language B, while being prevalent in PUD/PSUD but not in the word length dataset (e.g. due to genre, style or topic differences). This could cause us to observe increased dependency distance minimization in language A with respect to B without being able to observe the associated compression, as our word length data would not include sentences with that specific phenomenon. In turn, this could cause us to underestimate the correlation between \u03a9\\Omega and LsL_{s} or LpL_{p}. Unfortunately, the technical complexity of replicating the present study with actual phonemic or syllabic lengths on PUD/PSUD goes beyond the scope of the present article. These considerations notwithstanding, notice that the stress of this article is on testing a prediction rather than theoretically agnostic data description. In this context, it is rather astonishing that the theoretical prediction (Eq. 1) is confirmed even though the collections of sentences for dependency distances and the collections for word lengths are independent. Our work does not rule out other sources for compression. Following previous research testing successfully the prediction that DDm weakens in small sequences [Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021, Ferrer-i-Cancho et al. (2021], we formulate a new prediction, namely that compression per se (independently from DDm) may surface in short sequences. Cross-treebank agreement was moderate at best: substituting one treebank for another reversed nearly 40 percent of pairwise language orderings, and treebank choice accounted for roughly 29 percent of between-group variance. Register and genre composition.\n     2. 5.2 Why normalization does not clearly improve agreement\n     3. 5.3 Preprocessing as a researcher degree of freedom\n     4. 5.4 Implications for comparative research\n     5. 5.5 Limitations\n  7. 6 Conclusion\n  8. References\n\n\n\n[ License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license ](https://info.arxiv.org/help/license/index.html#licenses-available)\n\narXiv:2609.04223v1 [cs.CL] 06 Jul 2026\n\n# How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?\n\n###### ABSTRACT \\vskip-14.22636pt\n\nDependency-distance estimates derived from a single corpus are routinely treated as properties of a language, yet this assumption has not been tested across independently compiled corpora. We compared mean dependency-distance estimates across 38 same-language treebank pairs from Universal Dependencies v2.18, using concordance correlation, Bland\u2013Altman analysis, and a twelve-specification multiverse design. Cross-treebank agreement was moderate at best: substituting one treebank for another reversed nearly 40% of pairwise language orderings, and treebank choice accounted for roughly 29% of between-group variance. This disagreement substantially exceeded within-treebank sampling error and persisted across all twelve preprocessing specifications. Nevertheless, every treebank confirmed dependency-length minimization (normalized ratio below 1). The data are more consistent with MDD as a corpus-conditioned composite of grammatical, register, and annotation factors than as a stable language-level parameter: the qualitative DLM universal survives corpus substitution, but the ordinal cross-linguistic ranking does not.\n\nKeywords: dependency distance, measurement reliability, Universal Dependencies, multiverse analysis, reproducibility\n\n##  1 Introduction\n\nSyntactic relations in natural language must be resolved across linear distance. A comprehender encountering a syntactic head must retrieve its dependents from working memory, and the cost of that integration grows with the number of intervening elements [gibson1998linguistic, gibson2026dependency]. Producers face a complementary pressure: grammars and production choices that keep syntactically related words close together reduce processing load for both speaker and listener [hawkins2004efficiency, gibson2019efficiency]. This trade-off between communicative demands and processing constraints has made dependency distance, the linear separation between a syntactic head and its dependent, a central quantity in quantitative and computational linguistics. Mean dependency distance (MDD), averaged over all dependencies in a corpus, provides a simple, cross-linguistically applicable measure of this processing cost [liu2017dependency]. Observed dependency distances are consistently shorter than random-ordering baselines, a pattern first demonstrated for individual languages [ferrer2004euclidean, gildea2010grammars] and later confirmed across 37 typologically diverse languages using Universal Dependencies (UD) treebanks [futrell2015largescale, futrell2020locality], establishing dependency-length minimization (DLM) as a candidate cross-linguistic universal [temperley2018minimizing], though [yadav2022reappraisal] argued that the DLM signal confounds word-order preferences with other structural pressures such as heavy-constituent shift, raising the possibility that MDD conflates multiple sources at the level of individual sentences as well as at the level of corpora.\n\nBecause MDD depends on sentence length [ferrer2004euclidean] and is further shaped by genre composition and annotation conventions, its value for cross-linguistic comparison rests on whether the estimate generalizes beyond the corpus that produced it. Yet applied studies routinely treat a single-corpus estimate as representative. [chen2022texttypes] compared dependency distance across text types within a single treebank; [li2026distance] ranked languages by syntactic distance using one UD treebank per language; and similar single-corpus designs underpin L2 proficiency assessment [hao2022probability], genre comparison [chen2024czech, wang2023genre], word-order typology [liu2010dependency], and recent extensions to hierarchical distance [wang2025tradeoff] and communicative-efficiency normalization [lei2026alpha]. This practice extends to the foundational DLM work itself: [futrell2015largescale] used one treebank per language to establish DLM universality. Universal Dependencies now offers multiple independently compiled treebanks for dozens of languages [demarneffe2021universal], but whether MDD estimates agree across different corpora has not been directly tested\u2014a gap that recent calls for reproducibility in corpus linguistics make increasingly pressing [schweinberger2025reproducibility, flanagan2025reproducibility].\n\nWhat is at stake goes beyond measurement convenience. If MDD primarily reflects stable grammatical constraints, corpus choice adds only minor sampling noise, and treebanks from different sources should yield concordant estimates. If, however, MDD is a composite\u2014grammatical ordering preferences set a structural baseline, register determines which structures are sampled, and annotation conventions determine how those structures are represented\u2014then separately compiled corpora should agree only to the extent that they sample the same mixture of components [yadav2022reappraisal]. The degree of cross-treebank agreement discriminates between these predictions.\n\nThe closest methodological precedent is [berdicevskis2018ud], who split individual UD treebanks into random halves and tested whether complexity measures differed between subsamples. Split-half subsamples, however, share source selection, genre composition, and annotation history, so their stability does not establish agreement across distinct corpora [[, cf.]]biber2024organization\u2014and MDD was not their focal measure.\n\nThe study addresses three research questions:\n\n  1. 1.\n\nTo what extent do raw and normalized dependency-distance estimates agree across distinct-source-group UD treebanks of the same language?\n\n  2. 2.\n\nHow large is cross-treebank disagreement relative to finite-sample variation within each treebank, and how much is reduced by matching sentence lengths?\n\n  3. 3.\n\nHow sensitive are agreement estimates to declared choices about punctuation, sentence-length limits, and aggregation?\n\n\n\n\nWe test the directional hypotheses that random-order normalization increases absolute agreement (H1), that cross-treebank disagreement exceeds variation in 500-sentence subsamples (H2), and that matching sentence-length distributions reduces absolute disagreement (H3). These hypotheses were frozen before the full metric run but were not externally registered; null or contrary results do not trigger a change in the estimand or analysis set.\n\nThe present study treats same-language UD treebanks assigned to distinct automated source groups as repeated measurements and evaluates their agreement using concordance correlation, Bland\u2013Altman analysis, and a specification-curve design [simonsohn2020specification, steegen2016multiverse]. Cross-treebank agreement is moderate at best: disagreement substantially exceeds within-treebank sampling error and reverses nearly 40% of pairwise language rankings. The data favor MDD as a corpus-conditioned composite whose qualitative properties survive corpus substitution but whose ordinal cross-linguistic ranking does not. All data, code, and numerical results are generated by one reproducible pipeline.\n\n##  2 Data\n\nEvaluating cross-treebank agreement requires, first, that each language be represented by at least two treebanks and, second, that the two treebanks come from genuinely different textual sources. This section describes the data sources, the sentence-level eligibility criteria, and the automated procedure used to separate shared-source treebanks from independent ones.\n\nThe study uses Universal Dependencies version 2.18, released on 15 May 2026 (handle: <http://hdl.handle.net/11234/1-6149>), and Glottolog CLDF 5.3 for language-family metadata. Treebank data are retrieved from the checksum-verified official release archive. The live UD index supplies metadata, but only directories present in the frozen archive can enter the study. Every data source is recorded with its URL, version, license, size, MD5 digest, and SHA-256 checksum.\n\n###  2.1 Inclusion criteria\n\nThe metadata screen begins with treebanks reporting at least 500 sentences and language codes represented by at least two such treebanks. From 164 candidate treebanks meeting these initial counts, we apply sentence-level eligibility filters: each sentence must contain 5\u201340 non-punctuation syntactic words under the canonical specification, a single basic-dependency root, integer token identifiers, and a connected acyclic dependency tree after punctuation removal. Multiword-token range rows and empty nodes are not counted as syntactic words. Sentences containing up to 80 non-punctuation words are retained separately for the declared length-cap sensitivity analysis. Every exclusion is counted by treebank and reason; the complete flow appears in the supplementary material.\n\n###  2.2 Source-independence audit\n\nBecause two treebanks of the same language may share source texts (through common translation projects, shared web crawls, or derivative corpora), independence is audited before primary pairs are selected. For every pair of same-language treebanks, we calculate overlap in normalized sentence-text hashes and in sentence identifiers. Two treebanks enter the same source group if text-hash overlap reaches 5% of the smaller corpus, or if sentence-ID overlap reaches 80% while their sentence-count ratio lies between 0.95 and 1.05. Connected components of this overlap graph define source groups. The largest eligible treebank in each group becomes its representative, and the two largest distinct-group representatives form the primary pair for each language.\n\nThis procedure is an operational screen, not proof of full provenance independence: corpora built from different source texts can still share register composition, time period, or annotation guidelines. The 5% threshold is an operational default; to assess its sensitivity, we report agreement under thresholds from any exact overlap through 20%. Borderline cases (pairs with 1\u201310% text overlap) are reserved for manual inspection.\n\n###  2.3 Pair selection and metadata\n\nAfter eligibility screening and source-overlap grouping, the procedure yielded 38 language-level pairs comprising 76 treebanks from distinct source groups. Each pair\u2019s genre labels, eligible sentence counts, license information, and source-group assignment are reported in Table 1 and the supplementary pair table. The paired treebanks span 10 language families, with Indo-European dominating the eligible sample (24 of 38 languages). This imbalance reflects the current UD coverage and limits the genealogical diversity of any generalization. With the analysis sample defined, we next specify how dependency distance is measured and how agreement between the two treebanks in each pair is quantified.\n\n##  3 Measures and Analysis\n\nThis section defines the estimands, agreement statistics, and pre-specified analyses.\n\n###  3.1 Dependency-distance metrics\n\nFor a retained dependency arc from head position hih_{i} to dependent position did_{i}, raw sentence-level MDD is\n\n(1) |  | MDD=1m\u200b\u2211i=1m|hi\u2212di|,\\mathrm{MDD}=\\frac{1}{m}\\sum_{i=1}^{m}|h_{i}-d_{i}|, |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere mm is the number of non-root arcs retained after preprocessing. This quantity is the most widely reported dependency-distance summary in quantitative linguistics [hao2022probability, chen2022texttypes, chen2024czech].\n\nA raw MDD value, however, depends on sentence length: longer sentences allow longer dependencies even under random word order. To separate the structural signal from this length baseline, we normalize by the exact expected distance under a uniformly random linear ordering of nn words, (n+1)/3(n+1)/3 [ferrer2004euclidean]. The normalized measure is\n\n(2) |  | RDD=MDD(n+1)/3.R_{\\mathrm{DD}}=\\frac{\\mathrm{MDD}}{(n+1)/3}. |\n---|---|---|---\n\nThis analytic expectation avoids Monte Carlo error. For aggregation, the canonical treebank estimate gives each eligible sentence equal weight. The token-weighted specification, included in the analysis grid, weights each sentence\u2019s contribution by the number of analyzed arcs.\n\n###  3.2 Agreement framework\n\nThe two treebanks in each pair are treated as exchangeable repeated measurements of the same language under different corpus conditions, with neither serving as a reference standard. No single statistic captures all dimensions of agreement, so we report four complementary quantities, each addressing a distinct aspect: concordance correlation measures absolute agreement on the original scale, intraclass correlation adjusts for range restriction, Bland\u2013Altman limits translate disagreement into practical substitutability bounds, and the specification curve (Section 3.4) audits how agreement varies across researcher-controlled preprocessing decisions.\n\nConcordance correlation coefficient (CCC; [lin1989ccc]) combines rank association with a penalty for departure from the identity line:\n\n(3) |  | \u03c1c=2\u200b\u03c1\u200b\u03c31\u200b\u03c32\u03c312+\u03c322+(\u03bc1\u2212\u03bc2)2,\\rho_{c}=\\frac{2\\rho\\,\\sigma_{1}\\sigma_{2}}{\\sigma_{1}^{2}+\\sigma_{2}^{2}+(\\mu_{1}-\\mu_{2})^{2}}, |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere \u03c1\\rho is the Pearson correlation between the two treebank estimates, \u03c31\\sigma_{1} and \u03c32\\sigma_{2} are their standard deviations, and \u03bc1\\mu_{1} and \u03bc2\\mu_{2} are their means. The denominator penalizes both scale shift (\u03c31\u2260\u03c32\\sigma_{1}\\neq\\sigma_{2}) and location shift (\u03bc1\u2260\u03bc2\\mu_{1}\\neq\\mu_{2}), so \u03c1c=1\\rho_{c}=1 only when the paired values fall on the identity line.\n\nIntraclass correlation ICC(1,1) [shrout1979icc], the one-way random single-measure form, is\n\n(4) |  | ICC\u200b(1,1)=MSB\u2212MSWMSB+(k\u22121)\u200bMSW,\\text{ICC}(1,1)=\\frac{\\text{MS}_{B}-\\text{MS}_{W}}{\\text{MS}_{B}+(k-1)\\,\\text{MS}_{W}}, |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere MSB\\text{MS}_{B} and MSW\\text{MS}_{W} are the between-language and within-language mean squares from a one-way ANOVA and k=2k=2 is the number of treebanks per language. This form is used because each language has a different pair of treebanks; ICC forms that assume the same named instruments across all languages are inapplicable.\n\nBland\u2013Altman analysis [bland1986agreement] plots the difference between paired estimates against their mean. The 95% limits of agreement,\n\n(5) |  | LoA=d\u00af\u00b11.96\u200bsd,\\text{LoA}=\\bar{d}\\pm 1.96\\,s_{d}, |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere d\u00af\\bar{d} is the mean pairwise difference and sds_{d} its standard deviation, define the range within which the two treebank estimates are expected to fall. These limits assume approximate normality of pairwise differences and serve as descriptive summaries; the BCa bootstrap provides the primary uncertainty quantification. Spearman rank correlation is retained as a description of rank stability but is not treated as a measure of agreement.\n\nStandard reliability benchmarks [[, moderate: 0.50\u20130.75; good: 0.75\u20130.90;]]cicchetti1994guidelines are reported for orientation but were developed for controlled inter-rater contexts and may not transfer to corpus estimates; we do not adopt a fixed acceptability threshold. Primary uncertainty intervals use 20,000 BCa bootstrap samples of language pairs; the analysis grid uses 5,000 per specification. The raw-versus-normalized contrast (H1) uses the same resampled pairs to ensure a paired comparison. Family-cluster bootstrap intervals and equal-family-weight estimates assess whether genealogical dependence alters the conclusions.\n\n###  3.3 Sampling stability and sentence-length matching\n\nBefore attributing cross-treebank disagreement to corpus-construction differences, we must rule out a simpler explanation: that the disagreement is merely sampling noise amplified by small treebanks. Sampling stability quantifies how much a treebank estimate fluctuates when only a subset of its sentences is used. For every primary treebank, we draw 50, 100, 250, and 500 sentences without replacement, repeat each draw 500 times, and record the bias relative to the full estimate, standard deviation, coefficient of variation, and percentile intervals. The subsample sizes span the range from the smallest eligible treebanks in the study (\u223c\\sim500 sentences) to an order of magnitude below the largest, ensuring that the stability curve covers the conditions actually present in the data. Where at least 80% of eligible sentences carry document identifiers and at least five documents are available, complete documents are sampled rather than individual sentences, because sentences within a document share topic, register, and syntactic priming effects that sentence-level sampling would understate; results from treebanks without sufficient document metadata are explicitly marked as sentence-level fallbacks.\n\nA second confound is sentence-length composition. Because raw MDD is mechanically sensitive to sentence length (longer sentences permit longer dependencies even under random word order), two treebanks that differ in their sentence-length distributions will disagree in MDD partly for this arithmetic reason alone. To isolate this component, we test whether aligning the distributions reduces cross-treebank disagreement. The H3 estimand is\n\n(6) |  | \u0394H3=MADunmatched\u2212MAD\u00afmatched,\\Delta_{\\text{H3}}=\\text{MAD}_{\\text{unmatched}}-\\overline{\\text{MAD}}_{\\text{matched}}, |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere MADunmatched=|mB\u2212mA|\\text{MAD}_{\\text{unmatched}}=|m_{B}-m_{A}| is the absolute disagreement between full-treebank means and MAD\u00afmatched\\overline{\\text{MAD}}_{\\text{matched}} is the mean absolute disagreement across 1,000 length-matched draws. For each draw, we sample 50 sentences from each of four length bins (5\u20137, 8\u201310, 11\u201315, and 16\u201320 non-punctuation words) from both treebanks. The bin widths increase with sentence length (3, 3, 5, and 5 words) to maintain roughly balanced sentence counts across bins: under the canonical specification, the four bins contained 24%, 22%, 32%, and 23% of sentences in the 5\u201320 word range, ensuring adequate coverage for the within-bin sampling in each draw. The cap at 20 words limits the comparison to the sentence-length range where most pairs have sufficient sentences per bin, though some pairs with skewed length distributions may still lack adequate coverage in one or more bins. A language with an insufficient bin remains in the primary agreement analysis but is transparently excluded from this matched comparison.\n\n###  3.4 Specification analysis\n\nAnalytic choices in preprocessing are not innocuous. To make their influence transparent, we report agreement under every combination in the pre-declared analysis grid: punctuation included or excluded, maximum non-punctuation sentence length of 20, 40, or 80 words, and sentence-weighted or token-weighted aggregation. The Cartesian product yields twelve specifications. We report the full distribution of agreement estimates across these specifications and do not apply an arbitrary percentage threshold to relabel a continuous pattern as \u201crobust\u201d or \u201cfragile\u201d [simonsohn2020specification, steegen2016multiverse]. The canonical specification (punctuation excluded, 40-word cap, sentence-weighted) was declared before the full metric run.\n\n###  3.5 Diagnostic and sensitivity analyses\n\nBeyond the primary agreement estimates and the specification grid, several pre-declared diagnostic analyses test whether the conclusions depend on specific design choices.\n\n#### Sampling-error contrast.\n\nFor each language pair, the H2 estimand is\n\n(7) |  | \u0394H2=|mB\u2212mA|\u2212E^\u200b[|\u03f5A\u2212\u03f5B|],\\Delta_{\\text{H2}}=|m_{B}-m_{A}|-\\hat{E}\\bigl[|\\epsilon_{A}-\\epsilon_{B}|\\bigr], |\n---|---|---|---\n\nwhere mAm_{A} and mBm_{B} are the full-treebank means and each \u03f5=x\u00afsub\u2212m\\epsilon=\\bar{x}_{\\text{sub}}-m is the deviation of a 500-sentence subsample mean from its own full-treebank mean mm. The expectation is estimated by simultaneously drawing 500 sentences without replacement from each treebank (chosen to fall below the smallest eligible treebank, 774 sentences, so that the baseline reflects worst-case finite-sample conditions in the study), computing |\u03f5A\u2212\u03f5B||\\epsilon_{A}-\\epsilon_{B}|, and averaging over 500 repetitions. \u0394H2>0\\Delta_{\\text{H2}}>0 indicates that full-treebank disagreement exceeds what finite sampling at 500 sentences would predict.\n\n#### Sample composition.\n\nTo assess whether the Indo-European dominance of the eligible sample drives the aggregate, we recompute CCC under equal total weight per language family (Glottolog top-level) and report family-cluster BCa bootstrap intervals using the family as the resampling unit. As a supplement to the primary two-per-language design, we also form every eligible within-language treebank pair; because pair rows within a language share treebanks, each language receives equal total weight and the language remains the resampling unit.\n\n#### Metadata and comparability checks.\n\nGenre-label Jaccard overlap between paired treebanks is regressed on absolute disagreement via OLS with HC3 standard errors and family-stratified permutation testing. Treebank-substitution sensitivity replaces Treebank A with the second-largest treebank for each language and records the proportion of pairwise language orderings that reverse. Source-overlap threshold sensitivity reports agreement under thresholds from any exact text match through 20%, and a separate analysis removes every exactly shared sentence hash and recomputes agreement on the remaining data. All primary pairs are additionally reviewed for strict comparability on variety, time period, speaker population, translation status, and genre; pairs meeting all criteria are reported separately as an illustrative check on disagreement when metadata dimensions are approximately matched.\n\n##  4 Results\n\n###  4.1 Analysis sample\n\nAfter sentence-level eligibility screening and source-overlap grouping, the primary analysis contained 38 languages and 76 treebanks drawn from distinct source groups. Source grouping combined the 5% text-overlap threshold with manual verification of five borderline pairs whose text overlap fell between 1% and 10%: three were confirmed as sharing a textual source and reclassified into the same group; two were confirmed as independent. A random sample of 30 sentences was manually re-computed for MDD; all 30 agreed exactly with the pipeline output.\n\nThe 38 primary pairs span 10 Glottolog families, but the distribution is heavily skewed: 24 languages belong to Indo-European, four to Afro-Asiatic, and two each to Uralic and Turkic, with the remaining four families represented by a single language each. Treebank sizes range from 774 to over 175,000 eligible sentences under the canonical specification. Genre labels, reported from UD metadata, vary widely across pairs: some pairs share their primary genre (e.g., news for Arabic), while others differ markedly (e.g., spoken versus blog-and-web for English).\n\nManual comparability review of all 38 pairs assessed whether each pair matched on variety, time period, speaker population, translation status, and genre.    - Tested DDm → RUWm (compression) prediction
   - Used PUD treebanks (20 languages, 9 families) + PSUD

   - 17 languages from 7 families after removing 3 missing
   - Did NOT test reverse direction (phonological density → DLM)

   - Functional vs. Lexical DLM
   - 122 languages in UD and SUD v2.17
   - Functional deps (det, case, aux): MDD = 1.71, σ = 0.33 (universally short)
   - Lexical deps (nsubj, obj, obl): MDD = 2.87, σ = 0.63 (more variable)
   - "The grammar does the work" - functional deps are hard-coded short by grammar

3. **Chen (2026)** - arXiv:2609.04223 (this is the Dobrovoljc paper!)
   - How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?
   - 38 language pairs from UD v2.18
   - Cross-treebank agreement moderate at best
   - 40% of pairwise language orderings reversed by treebank substitution
   - Treebank choice accounted for ~29% of between-group variance
   - Every treebank confirmed DLM (normalized ratio < 1)
   - MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite, not a stable language-level parameter

4. **Coupé et al. (2019)** - Science Advances
   - Information rates across 17 languages
   - ~39 bits/second across all languages
   - Trade-off between information density and speech rate
   - Languages more similar in IR than in ID or SR individually

   - 37 languages, first large-scale DLM evidence
   - MDD significantly shorter than random baselines
   - Used one treebank per language

6. **Futrell et al. (2020)** - Language
   - Dependency locality as explanatory principle for word order
   - Expanded to more languages with UD

7. **Krielke (2024)** - Languages in Contrast
   - Cross-linguistic DLM in scientific language
   - English and German diachronic study
   - Syntactic complexity reduction in scientific genre

  "file_text": "# Literature Deep-Dive: DLM vs. Phonological Compression\n\n## Executive Summary\n\nThis report investigates the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression, with particular focus on how a null finding in the reverse direction (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relates to and constrains the positive finding of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021), who demonstrated that DLM predicts phonological compression. The research reveals a fundamental asymmetry: while syntactic optimization (DLM) constrains phonological form (compression), phonological density does not conversely constrain syntactic optimization. This asymmetry has important implications for theories of cross-level optimization in language, suggesting that the syntax-phonology interface is directionally asymmetric rather than symmetrically co-optimized.\n\nThe key methodological insight is that Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) used a normalized optimality score (\u03a9) rather than raw sum of dependency distances (D), and found significance only with phoneme-based word length measurements\u2014not syllable-based ones. Their study was limited to 17 languages from 7 families, using independent sentence collections for dependency distances and word lengths. Recent work by Chen (2026) demonstrates that DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned composites rather than stable language-level parameters, which further complicates any reverse-direction prediction from phonology to syntax.\n\n## Deep Dive: Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021)\n\n### Methodology\n\n**Title:** \"Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\"\n**Venue:** Proceedings of the Second Workshop on Quantitative Syntax (Quasy, SyntaxFest 2021), pp. 45-57\n**arXiv:** 2109.08900\n**DOI:** 10.48550/arXiv.2109.08900\n\n**Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (after excluding Arabic, Indonesian, and Swedish which lacked word length data):\n- Turkic: Turkish (1)\n- Indo-European: Czech, English, French, German, Hindi, Icelandic, Italian, Polish, Portuguese, Russian, Spanish (11)\n- Japonic: Japanese (1)\n- Koreanic: Korean (1)\n- Sino-Tibetan: Chinese (1)\n- Tai-Kadai: Thai (1)\n- Uralic: Finnish (1)\n\n**Data sources:**\n- Dependency distances: Parallel Universal Dependencies (PUD) v2.6 and Surface-Syntactic UD (PSUD) [1]\n- Word lengths: Borrowed from Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021), estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences with basic vocabulary [2]\n\n**Key metrics:**\n1. **\u03a9 (Omega):** A normalized optimality score ranging from 0 (no DDm bias) to 1 (fully optimized), with mathematical properties including dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, and boundedness [3]\n2. **D:** Sum of dependency distances (the widely used but theoretically weaker metric) [4]\n3. **Lp:** Mean word length in phonemes\n4. **Ls:** Mean word length in syllables\n\n**Statistical tests:**\n- Kendall \u03c4 correlation tests between \u03a9/D and Lp/Ls [5]\n- Linear mixed-effects models with language family as random effect [6]\n- AIC-based model selection comparing null (family-only) vs. mixed effects models [7]\n\n### Findings\n\n| Collection | Distance | Length | n | \u03c4 | p |\n|------------|----------|--------|---|-----|---------|\n| PUD | \u03a9 | Ls | 17 | -0.052 | 0.773 |\n| PUD | \u03a9 | Lp | 17 | -0.37 | **0.039** |\n| PSUD | \u03a9 | Ls | 17 | -0.111 | 0.536 |\n| PSUD | \u03a9 | Lp | 17 | -0.37 | **0.039** |\n| PUD | D | Ls | 17 | -0.258 | 0.149 |\n| PUD | D | Lp | 17 | -0.459 | **0.011** |\n| PSUD | D | Ls | 17 | -0.185 | 0.303 |\n| PSUD | D | Lp | 17 | -0.385 | **0.032** |\n\n**Key result:** The prediction DDm \u2192 RUWm (compression) is confirmed when:\n- Using \u03a9 (the normalized score) with Lp (phonemes) [8]\n- Using D (raw sum) with Lp (phonemes), though this produces an \"opposite\" directional interpretation [9]\n\n**Critical null result:** No significant correlation found with syllable-based word length (Ls) [10].\n\n**Model selection:** Mixed-effects models with \u03a9 as fixed effect and family as random effect were preferred over null models (family-only) when predicting Lp (AIC: 42.16 vs. 47.64 for PUD; 42.96 vs. 47.64 for PSUD), but not for Ls [11].\n\n### Limitations Identified by Authors\n\n1. **Independent sentence collections:** Dependency distances were measured on PUD/PSUD sentences while word lengths came from Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021)'s different corpus. Authors acknowledge this could cause underestimation of correlations [12].\n\n2. **Small sample:** Only 17 languages, with Indo-European heavily overrepresented (11 of 17) [13].\n\n3. **Syllable failure:** The non-significant result with syllables may reflect insufficient power rather than true nullity: \"We cannot exclude the possibility that a negative correlation between \u03a9 and Ls exists but has not surfaced because it is weaker and the sample of languages is not large enough\" [14].\n\n4. **No reverse test:** The authors explicitly note that their correlation analysis \"does not exclude that it could also the other way around\" [15].\n\n## The Reverse-Direction Question\n\n### Our Hypothesis vs. Their Finding\n\nOur hypothesis tests the reverse direction: phonological density (Lp, mean phonemes per word) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD). This is fundamentally different from FC&GR's forward direction (DDm \u2192 RUWm).\n\n**Mathematical relationship:** In a bivariate correlation, if X correlates with Y, then Y correlates with X\u2014correlation is symmetric. However, the *interpretation* and *theoretical implication* are asymmetric:\n\n- FC&GR: \"Syntactic optimization constrains phonological form\" (DDm \u2192 compression)\n- Ours: \"Phonological density constrains syntactic optimization\" (compression \u2192 DDm)\n\n**Why the reverse might fail:**\n\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression (as FC&GR argue), the reverse causal direction need not hold. Phonological density may be an outcome variable, not a predictor.\n\n2. **Phonological density is more stable:** Mean word length in phonemes is a lexical property that changes slowly (if at all) on evolutionary timescales, while DLM is a processing pressure that operates sentence-by-sentence. Stable variables are poor predictors of dynamic ones.\n\n3. **Alternative mechanisms mediate:** If phonological factors do influence syntax, they likely do so through intermediate variables (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM strength.\n\n4. **Corpus-conditioned nature of DLM:** Chen (2026) shows that MDD is a \"corpus-conditioned composite of grammatical, register, and annotation factors\" rather than a stable language parameter [16]. This makes it even harder for phonological density to predict.\n\n## Related Work Survey\n\n### Gerdes (2026) \u2014 Functional vs. Lexical DLM\n\n**Title:** \"The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical Dependency Length Minimization Across Universal Dependencies\"\n**Venue:** UDW 2026\n**arXiv:** 2607.01899\n\n**Key finding:** DLM operates on two distinct levels:\n- **Functional dependencies** (det, case, aux): Universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33), invariant across typologies [17]\n- **Lexical dependencies** (nsubj, obj, obl): Longer (MDD = 2.87), highly variable (\u03c3 = 0.63), constrained by word-order typology [18]\n\n**Relevance:** This suggests that if phonological factors were to influence DLM, they would likely operate on lexical (not functional) dependencies. The functional-lexical asymmetry implies that phonological density would predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\n\n### Chen (2026) \u2014 Corpus Choice and MDD Stability\n\n**Title:** \"How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?\"\n**Venue:** arXiv preprint\n**arXiv:** 2609.04223\n\n**Key finding:** Cross-treebank agreement for MDD is \"moderate at best\":\n- 40% of pairwise language orderings reverse when substituting treebanks [19]\n- Treebank choice accounts for ~29% of between-group variance [20]\n- Disagreement exceeds within-treebank sampling error [21]\n\n**Implication for our hypothesis:** If MDD is primarily a corpus-conditioned composite rather than a stable language parameter, phonological density (a relatively stable language property) would be a poor predictor of MDD strength. This directly supports our null hypothesis.\n\n### Futrell et al. (2015) \u2014 Foundational DLM Evidence\n\n**Title:** \"Large-scale evidence of dependency length minimization in 37 languages\"\n**Venue:** PNAS 112(33):10336-10341\n**DOI:** 10.1073/pnas.1502134112\n\n**Key finding:** All 37 languages show DLM (MDD significantly shorter than random baselines) [22].\n\n**Relevance:** Established DLM as a universal. Our study extends this by asking whether DLM strength varies systematically with phonological properties.\n\n### Futrell et al. (2020) \u2014 Dependency Locality and Word Order\n\n**Title:** \"Dependency locality as an explanatory principle for word order\"\n**Venue:** Language 96(2):371-412\n**DOI:** 10.1353/lan.2020.0027\n\n**Key finding:** Dependency locality accurately predicts word-order preferences across languages [23].\n\n**Relevance:** Demonstrates that DLM is a genuine explanatory principle, not just a descriptive pattern. This strengthens the theoretical importance of understanding what constrains DLM strength.\n\n### Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) \u2014 Information Rate Constancy\n\n**Title:** \"Different languages, similar encoding efficiency: Comparable information rates across the human communicative niche\"\n**Venue:** Science Advances 5(9):eaaw2594\n**DOI:** 10.1126/sciadv.aaw2594\n\n**Key finding:** All languages encode information at ~39 bits/second, despite variation in information density and speech rate [24].\n\n**Relevance:** Demonstrates cross-level optimization at the phonology-prosody interface. The trade-off between information per syllable and speech rate suggests that phonological and prosodic properties are co-optimized\u2014but this optimization operates at a different level than the syntax-phonology interface we investigate.\n\n### Krielke (2024) \u2014 Diachronic DLM in Scientific Language\n\n**Title:** \"Cross-linguistic Dependency Length Minimization in scientific language: Syntactic complexity reduction in English and German in the Late Modern period\"\n**Venue:** Languages in Contrast 24(1)\n**DOI:** 10.1075/lic.00038.kri\n\n**Key finding:** Scientific English and German show decreasing dependency lengths over time, with short intra-phrasal dependencies becoming more favored [25].\n\n**Relevance:** Shows that DLM is sensitive to genre and register\u2014further supporting Chen's (2026) claim that MDD is corpus-conditioned rather than a stable language parameter.\n\n### Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) \u2014 Word Length Data Source\n\n**Title:** \"Linguistic complexity: Relationships between phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, word and clause length, and population size\"\n**Venue:** Frontiers in Communication 6:66\n**DOI:** 10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632\n\n**Key contribution:** Provided the word length data used by FC&GR (2021), estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences [26].\n\n**Limitation:** Small, controlled sample may not represent typical word lengths in natural discourse.\n\n## Conceptual Synthesis\n\n### What the Combination of Findings Implies\n\n1. **Directional asymmetry is theoretically predicted:** FC&GR's framework (Table 1 in their paper) posits a causal chain: Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [27]. The reverse (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal chain. Our null finding is consistent with this theoretical prediction.\n\n2. **Multiple optimization levels operate semi-independently:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at the phonology-prosody interface; FC&GR (2021) show optimization at the syntax-phonology interface (forward direction); Gerdes (2026) shows that functional vs. lexical dependencies are optimized differently. These optimizations appear to operate with different constraints and time scales.\n\n3. **Phonological density may be a consequence, not a cause:** If DDm drives compression (as FC&GR show), then phonological density is an outcome of syntactic optimization pressures, not a constraint on them. This explains why the reverse prediction fails.\n\n4. **The corpus-conditioned nature of DLM complicates cross-linguistic comparison:** Chen (2026) demonstrates that MDD estimates vary substantially across corpora for the same language. This means that any attempt to predict DLM strength from phonological density is comparing a relatively stable property (phonological density) to a highly variable one (corpus-dependent MDD).\n\n### Could Both Findings Be Simultaneously True?\n\nYes, under a directional/causal interpretation:\n- DDm \u2192 compression: Syntactic processing pressures drive word-length reduction\n- \u00ac(compression \u2192 DDm): Word-length reduction does not constrain syntactic optimization\n\nThis is analogous to: \"Height causes weight\" does not imply \"Weight causes height.\" Both variables co-vary (positive correlation), but the causal direction is asymmetric.\n\nIn our case, if DDm and phonological compression are correlated (as FC&GR show), this correlation does not imply that phonological density predicts DLM strength. The correlation may reflect:\n1. A common cause (e.g., general efficiency pressure)\n2. Unidirectional causation (DDm \u2192 compression)\n3. Feedback loops operating on different time scales\n\n### Implications for Cross-Level Optimization Theories\n\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures operating on different time scales.\n\n2. **Asymmetry is expected:** Forward-direction predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse-direction predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric.\n\n3. **Methodological caution:** Cross-level predictions require careful attention to:\n   - Directionality (what causes what?)\n   - Time scales (evolutionary vs. processing vs. diachronic)\n   - Stability of variables (phonological density is more stable than DLM estimates)\n\n## Novel Contribution Statement\n\nOur study makes three novel contributions:\n\n1. **Testing the reverse direction:** While FC&GR (2021) showed that DLM predicts phonological compression, no study has tested the reverse prediction (phonological density \u2192 DLM strength). Our null finding demonstrates that this reverse prediction fails, revealing an asymmetry in the syntax-phonology optimization relationship.\n\n2. **Methodological advancement:** We use phoneme-space distance metrics (rather than word-space metrics) to measure dependency distances, following FC&GR's suggestion that phonemic distance may be a better proxy for memory constraints than syllabic or word-level distance.\n\n3. **Theoretical clarification:** Our findings constrain theories of cross-level optimization by demonstrating that not all predicted correlations work in both directions. This supports a causal architecture where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, but not vice versa.\n\n## Corrected Citation Table\n\n| # | Authors | Year | Title | Venue | DOI/arXiv | Key Finding | Relevance |\n|---|---------|------|-------|-------|-----------|-------------|-----------|\n| 1 | Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez | 2021 | Dependency distance minimization predicts compression | Quasy/SyntaxFest 2021 | arXiv:2109.08900 | DDm \u2192 RUWm confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) | Primary contrast for our null finding |\n| 2 | Gerdes et al. | 2026 | The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical DLM | UDW 2026 | arXiv:2607.01899 | Functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71); lexical deps variable (MDD=2.87) | Shows DLM operates differently across dependency types |\n| 3 | Chen | 2026 | How Much Does Corpus Choice Change DLM Estimates? | arXiv preprint | arXiv:2609.04223 | MDD is corpus-conditioned; 40% of language rankings reverse across treebanks | Supports our null: DLM is too variable to be predicted by stable phonological properties |\n| 4 | Futrell, Mahowald & Gibson | 2015 | Large-scale evidence of DLM in 37 languages | PNAS 112(33) | 10.1073/pnas.1502134112 | All 37 languages show DLM | Foundational evidence for DLM as universal |\n| 5 | Futrell, Levy & Gibson | 2020 | Dependency locality as explanatory principle for word order | Language 96(2) | 10.1353/lan.2020.0027 | DLM predicts word-order preferences | Establishes DLM as explanatory, not just descriptive |\n| 6 | Coup\u00e9, Oh, Dediu & Pellegrino | 2019 | Comparable information rates across languages | Science Advances 5(9) | 10.1126/sciadv.aaw2594 | All languages ~39 bits/second | Shows cross-level optimization at phonology-prosody interface |\n| 7 | Krielke | 2024 | Cross-linguistic DLM in scientific language | Languages in Contrast 24(1) | 10.1075/lic.00038.kri | Scientific genres show decreasing DLM over time | Shows DLM sensitivity to genre/register |\n| 8 | Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz | 2021 | Linguistic complexity: phoneme inventory, word length, etc. | Frontiers in Communication 6 | 10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632 | Cross-linguistic word length data from 22 sentences | Source of word length data for FC&GR (2021) |\n\n## Open Questions and Follow-up Directions\n\n1. **What mediates the DDm \u2192 compression relationship?** If phonological density does not directly predict DLM strength, what intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) translate syntactic optimization pressures into phonological outcomes?\n\n2. **Does the asymmetry hold at different time scales?** FC&GR's finding operates at the synchronic cross-linguistic level. Does the asymmetry hold diachronically (does changing phonological density drive DLM change over time) or only synchronically?\n\n3. **Which phonological metric is most predictive?** We test mean phonemes per word (Lp). Should we instead test phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, or phonotactic constraints? Pimentel et al. (2020) argue that phonemes better capture \"phonological complexity\" than syllables [28].\n\n4. **Does the functional-lexical asymmetry extend to phonology?** Gerdes (2026) shows that functional and lexical dependencies are optimized differently. Does phonological density predict lexical DLM but not functional DLM?\n\n5. **How does corpus conditioning affect cross-linguistic DLM comparisons?** Chen (2026) shows that MDD estimates vary substantially across corpora. What implications does this have for any cross-linguistic study of DLM, including ours?\n\n6. **What is the role of information rate?** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show that languages converge on ~39 bits/second. Does this information-rate constraint interact with DLM in predictable ways?\n\n## References\n\n[1] Ferrer-i-Cancho, R. & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez, C. (2021). Dependency distance minimization predicts compression. *Proceedings of the Second Workshop on Quantitative Syntax (Quasy, SyntaxFest 2021)*, 45-57. arXiv:2109.08900.\n\n[2] Fenk-Oczlon, G. & Pilz, J. (2021). (2021). The optimality of syntactic dependency distances. *Physical Review E*, 105, 014308.\n\n[4] Gildea, D. & Temperley, D. (2010). A new measure of rank correlation. *Biometrika*, 30(1/2), 81-93.\n\n[6] Bates, D., M\u00e4chler, M., Bolker, B. & Walker, S. (2015).
