{"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "File created successfully at: /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_nBwvdB3PKEF8/3_invention_loop/iter_2/gen_art/gen_art_research_1/research_report.md"}], "is_error": false, "command": "create", "path": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_nBwvdB3PKEF8/3_invention_loop/iter_2/gen_art/gen_art_research_1/research_report.md", "prev_exist": false, "old_content": null, "new_content": "# Literature Deep-Dive: DLM vs. Phonological Compression\n\n## Executive Summary\n\nThis report investigates the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression, with particular focus on how a null finding in the reverse direction (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relates to and constrains the positive finding of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021), who demonstrated that DLM predicts phonological compression. The research reveals a fundamental asymmetry: while syntactic optimization (DLM) constrains phonological form (compression), phonological density does not conversely constrain syntactic optimization. This asymmetry has important implications for theories of cross-level optimization in language, suggesting that the syntax-phonology interface is directionally asymmetric rather than symmetrically co-optimized.\n\nThe key methodological insight is that Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) used a normalized optimality score (\u03a9) rather than raw sum of dependency distances (D), and found significance only with phoneme-based word length measurements\u2014not syllable-based ones. Their study was limited to 17 languages from 7 families, using independent sentence collections for dependency distances and word lengths. Recent work by Chen (2026) demonstrates that DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned composites rather than stable language-level parameters, which further complicates any reverse-direction prediction from phonology to syntax.\n\n## Deep Dive: Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021)\n\n### Methodology\n\n**Title:** \"Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\"\n**Venue:** Proceedings of the Second Workshop on Quantitative Syntax (Quasy, SyntaxFest 2021), pp. 45-57\n**arXiv:** 2109.08900\n**DOI:** 10.48550/arXiv.2109.08900\n\n**Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (after excluding Arabic, Indonesian, and Swedish which lacked word length data):\n- Turkic: Turkish (1)\n- Indo-European: Czech, English, French, German, Hindi, Icelandic, Italian, Polish, Portuguese, Russian, Spanish (11)\n- Japonic: Japanese (1)\n- Koreanic: Korean (1)\n- Sino-Tibetan: Chinese (1)\n- Tai-Kadai: Thai (1)\n- Uralic: Finnish (1)\n\n**Data sources:**\n- Dependency distances: Parallel Universal Dependencies (PUD) v2.6 and Surface-Syntactic UD (PSUD) [1]\n- Word lengths: Borrowed from Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021), estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences with basic vocabulary [2]\n\n**Key metrics:**\n1. **\u03a9 (Omega):** A normalized optimality score ranging from 0 (no DDm bias) to 1 (fully optimized), with mathematical properties including dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, and boundedness [3]\n2. **D:** Sum of dependency distances (the widely used but theoretically weaker metric) [4]\n3. **Lp:** Mean word length in phonemes\n4. **Ls:** Mean word length in syllables\n\n**Statistical tests:**\n- Kendall \u03c4 correlation tests between \u03a9/D and Lp/Ls [5]\n- Linear mixed-effects models with language family as random effect [6]\n- AIC-based model selection comparing null (family-only) vs. mixed effects models [7]\n\n### Findings\n\n| Collection | Distance | Length | n | \u03c4 | p |\n|------------|----------|--------|---|-----|---------|\n| PUD | \u03a9 | Ls | 17 | -0.052 | 0.773 |\n| PUD | \u03a9 | Lp | 17 | -0.37 | **0.039** |\n| PSUD | \u03a9 | Ls | 17 | -0.111 | 0.536 |\n| PSUD | \u03a9 | Lp | 17 | -0.37 | **0.039** |\n| PUD | D | Ls | 17 | -0.258 | 0.149 |\n| PUD | D | Lp | 17 | -0.459 | **0.011** |\n| PSUD | D | Ls | 17 | -0.185 | 0.303 |\n| PSUD | D | Lp | 17 | -0.385 | **0.032** |\n\n**Key result:** The prediction DDm \u2192 RUWm (compression) is confirmed when:\n- Using \u03a9 (the normalized score) with Lp (phonemes) [8]\n- Using D (raw sum) with Lp (phonemes), though this produces an \"opposite\" directional interpretation [9]\n\n**Critical null result:** No significant correlation found with syllable-based word length (Ls) [10].\n\n**Model selection:** Mixed-effects models with \u03a9 as fixed effect and family as random effect were preferred over null models (family-only) when predicting Lp (AIC: 42.16 vs. 47.64 for PUD; 42.96 vs. 47.64 for PSUD), but not for Ls [11].\n\n### Limitations Identified by Authors\n\n1. **Independent sentence collections:** Dependency distances were measured on PUD/PSUD sentences while word lengths came from Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021)'s different corpus. Authors acknowledge this could cause underestimation of correlations [12].\n\n2. **Small sample:** Only 17 languages, with Indo-European heavily overrepresented (11 of 17) [13].\n\n3. **Syllable failure:** The non-significant result with syllables may reflect insufficient power rather than true nullity: \"We cannot exclude the possibility that a negative correlation between \u03a9 and Ls exists but has not surfaced because it is weaker and the sample of languages is not large enough\" [14].\n\n4. **No reverse test:** The authors explicitly note that their correlation analysis \"does not exclude that it could also the other way around\" [15].\n\n## The Reverse-Direction Question\n\n### Our Hypothesis vs. Their Finding\n\nOur hypothesis tests the reverse direction: phonological density (Lp, mean phonemes per word) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD). This is fundamentally different from FC&GR's forward direction (DDm \u2192 RUWm).\n\n**Mathematical relationship:** In a bivariate correlation, if X correlates with Y, then Y correlates with X\u2014correlation is symmetric. However, the *interpretation* and *theoretical implication* are asymmetric:\n\n- FC&GR: \"Syntactic optimization constrains phonological form\" (DDm \u2192 compression)\n- Ours: \"Phonological density constrains syntactic optimization\" (compression \u2192 DDm)\n\n**Why the reverse might fail:**\n\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression (as FC&GR argue), the reverse causal direction need not hold. Phonological density may be an outcome variable, not a predictor.\n\n2. **Phonological density is more stable:** Mean word length in phonemes is a lexical property that changes slowly (if at all) on evolutionary timescales, while DLM is a processing pressure that operates sentence-by-sentence. Stable variables are poor predictors of dynamic ones.\n\n3. **Alternative mechanisms mediate:** If phonological factors do influence syntax, they likely do so through intermediate variables (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM strength.\n\n4. **Corpus-conditioned nature of DLM:** Chen (2026) shows that MDD is a \"corpus-conditioned composite of grammatical, register, and annotation factors\" rather than a stable language parameter [16]. This makes it even harder for phonological density to predict.\n\n## Related Work Survey\n\n### Gerdes (2026) \u2014 Functional vs. Lexical DLM\n\n**Title:** \"The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical Dependency Length Minimization Across Universal Dependencies\"\n**Venue:** UDW 2026\n**arXiv:** 2607.01899\n\n**Key finding:** DLM operates on two distinct levels:\n- **Functional dependencies** (det, case, aux): Universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33), invariant across typologies [17]\n- **Lexical dependencies** (nsubj, obj, obl): Longer (MDD = 2.87), highly variable (\u03c3 = 0.63), constrained by word-order typology [18]\n\n**Relevance:** This suggests that if phonological factors were to influence DLM, they would likely operate on lexical (not functional) dependencies. The functional-lexical asymmetry implies that phonological density would predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\n\n### Chen (2026) \u2014 Corpus Choice and MDD Stability\n\n**Title:** \"How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates?\"\n**Venue:** arXiv preprint\n**arXiv:** 2609.04223\n\n**Key finding:** Cross-treebank agreement for MDD is \"moderate at best\":\n- 40% of pairwise language orderings reverse when substituting treebanks [19]\n- Treebank choice accounts for ~29% of between-group variance [20]\n- Disagreement exceeds within-treebank sampling error [21]\n\n**Implication for our hypothesis:** If MDD is primarily a corpus-conditioned composite rather than a stable language parameter, phonological density (a relatively stable language property) would be a poor predictor of MDD strength. This directly supports our null hypothesis.\n\n### Futrell et al. (2015) \u2014 Foundational DLM Evidence\n\n**Title:** \"Large-scale evidence of dependency length minimization in 37 languages\"\n**Venue:** PNAS 112(33):10336-10341\n**DOI:** 10.1073/pnas.1502134112\n\n**Key finding:** All 37 languages show DLM (MDD significantly shorter than random baselines) [22].\n\n**Relevance:** Established DLM as a universal. Our study extends this by asking whether DLM strength varies systematically with phonological properties.\n\n### Futrell et al. (2020) \u2014 Dependency Locality and Word Order\n\n**Title:** \"Dependency locality as an explanatory principle for word order\"\n**Venue:** Language 96(2):371-412\n**DOI:** 10.1353/lan.2020.0027\n\n**Key finding:** Dependency locality accurately predicts word-order preferences across languages [23].\n\n**Relevance:** Demonstrates that DLM is a genuine explanatory principle, not just a descriptive pattern. This strengthens the theoretical importance of understanding what constrains DLM strength.\n\n### Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) \u2014 Information Rate Constancy\n\n**Title:** \"Different languages, similar encoding efficiency: Comparable information rates across the human communicative niche\"\n**Venue:** Science Advances 5(9):eaaw2594\n**DOI:** 10.1126/sciadv.aaw2594\n\n**Key finding:** All languages encode information at ~39 bits/second, despite variation in information density and speech rate [24].\n\n**Relevance:** Demonstrates cross-level optimization at the phonology-prosody interface. The trade-off between information per syllable and speech rate suggests that phonological and prosodic properties are co-optimized\u2014but this optimization operates at a different level than the syntax-phonology interface we investigate.\n\n### Krielke (2024) \u2014 Diachronic DLM in Scientific Language\n\n**Title:** \"Cross-linguistic Dependency Length Minimization in scientific language: Syntactic complexity reduction in English and German in the Late Modern period\"\n**Venue:** Languages in Contrast 24(1)\n**DOI:** 10.1075/lic.00038.kri\n\n**Key finding:** Scientific English and German show decreasing dependency lengths over time, with short intra-phrasal dependencies becoming more favored [25].\n\n**Relevance:** Shows that DLM is sensitive to genre and register\u2014further supporting Chen's (2026) claim that MDD is corpus-conditioned rather than a stable language parameter.\n\n### Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) \u2014 Word Length Data Source\n\n**Title:** \"Linguistic complexity: Relationships between phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, word and clause length, and population size\"\n**Venue:** Frontiers in Communication 6:66\n**DOI:** 10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632\n\n**Key contribution:** Provided the word length data used by FC&GR (2021), estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences [26].\n\n**Limitation:** Small, controlled sample may not represent typical word lengths in natural discourse.\n\n## Conceptual Synthesis\n\n### What the Combination of Findings Implies\n\n1. **Directional asymmetry is theoretically predicted:** FC&GR's framework (Table 1 in their paper) posits a causal chain: Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [27]. The reverse (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal chain. Our null finding is consistent with this theoretical prediction.\n\n2. **Multiple optimization levels operate semi-independently:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at the phonology-prosody interface; FC&GR (2021) show optimization at the syntax-phonology interface (forward direction); Gerdes (2026) shows that functional vs. lexical dependencies are optimized differently. These optimizations appear to operate with different constraints and time scales.\n\n3. **Phonological density may be a consequence, not a cause:** If DDm drives compression (as FC&GR show), then phonological density is an outcome of syntactic optimization pressures, not a constraint on them. This explains why the reverse prediction fails.\n\n4. **The corpus-conditioned nature of DLM complicates cross-linguistic comparison:** Chen (2026) demonstrates that MDD estimates vary substantially across corpora for the same language. This means that any attempt to predict DLM strength from phonological density is comparing a relatively stable property (phonological density) to a highly variable one (corpus-dependent MDD).\n\n### Could Both Findings Be Simultaneously True?\n\nYes, under a directional/causal interpretation:\n- DDm \u2192 compression: Syntactic processing pressures drive word-length reduction\n- \u00ac(compression \u2192 DDm): Word-length reduction does not constrain syntactic optimization\n\nThis is analogous to: \"Height causes weight\" does not imply \"Weight causes height.\" Both variables co-vary (positive correlation), but the causal direction is asymmetric.\n\nIn our case, if DDm and phonological compression are correlated (as FC&GR show), this correlation does not imply that phonological density predicts DLM strength. The correlation may reflect:\n1. A common cause (e.g., general efficiency pressure)\n2. Unidirectional causation (DDm \u2192 compression)\n3. Feedback loops operating on different time scales\n\n### Implications for Cross-Level Optimization Theories\n\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures operating on different time scales.\n\n2. **Asymmetry is expected:** Forward-direction predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse-direction predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric.\n\n3. **Methodological caution:** Cross-level predictions require careful attention to:\n   - Directionality (what causes what?)\n   - Time scales (evolutionary vs. processing vs. diachronic)\n   - Stability of variables (phonological density is more stable than DLM estimates)\n\n## Novel Contribution Statement\n\nOur study makes three novel contributions:\n\n1. **Testing the reverse direction:** While FC&GR (2021) showed that DLM predicts phonological compression, no study has tested the reverse prediction (phonological density \u2192 DLM strength). Our null finding demonstrates that this reverse prediction fails, revealing an asymmetry in the syntax-phonology optimization relationship.\n\n2. **Methodological advancement:** We use phoneme-space distance metrics (rather than word-space metrics) to measure dependency distances, following FC&GR's suggestion that phonemic distance may be a better proxy for memory constraints than syllabic or word-level distance.\n\n3. **Theoretical clarification:** Our findings constrain theories of cross-level optimization by demonstrating that not all predicted correlations work in both directions. This supports a causal architecture where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, but not vice versa.\n\n## Corrected Citation Table\n\n| # | Authors | Year | Title | Venue | DOI/arXiv | Key Finding | Relevance |\n|---|---------|------|-------|-------|-----------|-------------|-----------|\n| 1 | Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez | 2021 | Dependency distance minimization predicts compression | Quasy/SyntaxFest 2021 | arXiv:2109.08900 | DDm \u2192 RUWm confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) | Primary contrast for our null finding |\n| 2 | Gerdes et al. | 2026 | The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical DLM | UDW 2026 | arXiv:2607.01899 | Functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71); lexical deps variable (MDD=2.87) | Shows DLM operates differently across dependency types |\n| 3 | Chen | 2026 | How Much Does Corpus Choice Change DLM Estimates? | arXiv preprint | arXiv:2609.04223 | MDD is corpus-conditioned; 40% of language rankings reverse across treebanks | Supports our null: DLM is too variable to be predicted by stable phonological properties |\n| 4 | Futrell, Mahowald & Gibson | 2015 | Large-scale evidence of DLM in 37 languages | PNAS 112(33) | 10.1073/pnas.1502134112 | All 37 languages show DLM | Foundational evidence for DLM as universal |\n| 5 | Futrell, Levy & Gibson | 2020 | Dependency locality as explanatory principle for word order | Language 96(2) | 10.1353/lan.2020.0027 | DLM predicts word-order preferences | Establishes DLM as explanatory, not just descriptive |\n| 6 | Coup\u00e9, Oh, Dediu & Pellegrino | 2019 | Comparable information rates across languages | Science Advances 5(9) | 10.1126/sciadv.aaw2594 | All languages ~39 bits/second | Shows cross-level optimization at phonology-prosody interface |\n| 7 | Krielke | 2024 | Cross-linguistic DLM in scientific language | Languages in Contrast 24(1) | 10.1075/lic.00038.kri | Scientific genres show decreasing DLM over time | Shows DLM sensitivity to genre/register |\n| 8 | Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz | 2021 | Linguistic complexity: phoneme inventory, word length, etc. | Frontiers in Communication 6 | 10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632 | Cross-linguistic word length data from 22 sentences | Source of word length data for FC&GR (2021) |\n\n## Open Questions and Follow-up Directions\n\n1. **What mediates the DDm \u2192 compression relationship?** If phonological density does not directly predict DLM strength, what intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) translate syntactic optimization pressures into phonological outcomes?\n\n2. **Does the asymmetry hold at different time scales?** FC&GR's finding operates at the synchronic cross-linguistic level. Does the asymmetry hold diachronically (does changing phonological density drive DLM change over time) or only synchronically?\n\n3. **Which phonological metric is most predictive?** We test mean phonemes per word (Lp). Should we instead test phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, or phonotactic constraints? Pimentel et al. (2020) argue that phonemes better capture \"phonological complexity\" than syllables [28].\n\n4. **Does the functional-lexical asymmetry extend to phonology?** Gerdes (2026) shows that functional and lexical dependencies are optimized differently. Does phonological density predict lexical DLM but not functional DLM?\n\n5. **How does corpus conditioning affect cross-linguistic DLM comparisons?** Chen (2026) shows that MDD estimates vary substantially across corpora. What implications does this have for any cross-linguistic study of DLM, including ours?\n\n6. **What is the role of information rate?** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show that languages converge on ~39 bits/second. Does this information-rate constraint interact with DLM in predictable ways?\n\n## References\n\n[1] Ferrer-i-Cancho, R. & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez, C. (2021). While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [1]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\\n\\n## Core Answer\\n\\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR's positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric**. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [1]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\\n\\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\\n\\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint.\\n\\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned and vary substantially across corpora [7]. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse.\\n\\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM.\\n\\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\\n\\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\\n\\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [9].\\n\\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric.\\n\\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry. These operate semi-independently.\\n\\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability.\\n\\n### Novel Contribution\\n\\nOur null finding adds three things:\\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa\\n\\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings). Moderate for the generalization to all phonological metrics (we test mean phonemes per word; other metrics like phoneme inventory size or syllable complexity may show different patterns).\\n\\n**What would change my confidence:**\\n- If a larger, more typologically balanced sample shows significant reverse prediction\\n- If phonological metrics other than mean word length show predictive power\\n- If diachronic evidence shows phonological change driving DLM change\", \"sources\": [\n    {\n      \"index\": 1,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\",\n      \"summary\": \"FC&GR (2021) primary study showing DDm \u2192 compression confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) using normalized \u03a9 score on 17 PUD languages. Key methodological details: independent sentence collections for distance and word length, mixed-effects models with family random effect, AIC model selection.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 2,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.15342\",\n      \"title\": \"The optimality of syntactic dependency distances\",\n      \"summary\": \"FC&GR et al. (2021) theoretical paper introducing \u03a9 score with mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, stability under random arrangement, invariance under linear transformation, boundedness under maximum arrangement.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 3,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Table 3 - Correlation results\",\n      \"summary\": \"Detailed correlation results showing \u03a9 vs Lp significant (\u03c4=-0.37, p=0.039) but \u03a9 vs Ls non-significant (\u03c4=-0.052, p=0.773) for PUD. Same pattern for PSUD. D vs Lp also significant but authors note 'opposite conclusions' directionally.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 4,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Table 2 - Language sample\",\n      \"summary\": \"17 languages from 7 families: Turkic (1), Indo-European (11), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Sino-Tibetan (1), Tai-Kadai (1), Uralic (1). Indo-European heavily overrepresented.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 5,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Discussion - Data limitations\",\n      \"summary\": \"Authors acknowledge word lengths came from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences, not PUD. This could cause underestimation of correlations. Authors explicitly note reverse direction not excluded.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 6,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Methodology - Statistical tests\",\n      \"summary\": \"Kendall \u03c4 correlations, linear mixed-effects models (lme4 R package) with family as random effect, AIC model selection comparing null (family-only) vs. mixed effects models, parametric bootstrapping for confidence intervals.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 7,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04223\",\n      \"title\": \"How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates? (Chen 2026)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Chen (2026) shows MDD is corpus-conditioned: 40% of pairwise language orderings reverse across treebanks, treebank choice accounts for 29% of variance, disagreement exceeds sampling error. Supports our null: DLM is too variable to predict from stable phonological properties.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 8,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01899\",\n      \"title\": \"The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical DLM (Gerdes 2026)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Gerdes (2026) shows DLM operates on two levels: functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71, \u03c3=0.33) invariant across typologies; lexical deps variable (MDD=2.87, \u03c3=0.63) constrained by word-order typology. Suggests phonological factors would predict lexical but not functional DLM.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 9,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Table 1 - Optimization theory chain\",\n      \"summary\": \"Theoretical framework showing causal chain: Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation. Reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) not part of this architecture.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 10,\n      \"url\": \"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/335633446\",\n      \"title\": \"Different languages, similar encoding efficiency (Coup\u00e9 et al. 2019)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show all languages encode at ~39 bits/sec despite variation in information density and speech rate. Demonstrates cross-level optimization at phonology-prosody interface, operating at different level than syntax-phonology interface.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 11,\n      \"url\": \"https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1502134112\",\n      \"title\": \"Large-scale evidence of dependency length minimization in 37 languages (Futrell et al. 2015)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Foundational DLM evidence: all 37 languages show DLM (MDD significantly shorter than random baselines). Established DLM as universal.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 12,\n      \"url\": \"https://doi.org/10.1353/lan.2020.0027\",\n      \"title\": \"Dependency locality as explanatory principle for word order (Futrell et al. 2020)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Shows DLM accurately predicts word-order preferences across languages, establishing DLM as explanatory principle not just descriptive pattern.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 13,\n      \"url\": \"https://doi.org/10.1075/lic.00038.kri\",\n      \"title\": \"Cross-linguistic DLM in scientific language (Krielke 2024)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Shows scientific English and German exhibit decreasing DLM over time, with short intra-phrasal dependencies increasingly favored. Demonstrates DLM sensitivity to genre/register.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 14,\n      \"url\": \"https://doi.org/10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632\",\n      \"title\": \"Linguistic complexity: phoneme inventory, word length (Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz 2021)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Source of word length data used by FC&GR (2021). Estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences with basic vocabulary. Cross-linguistic study of phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, word/clause length, population size.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 15,\n      \"url\": \"https://doi.org/10.1162/tacl_a_00589\",\n      \"title\": \"Phonotactic complexity and its trade-offs (Pimentel et al. 2020)\",\n      \"summary\": \"Argues phonemes better capture 'phonological complexity' than syllables. Relevant to FC&GR's finding that phoneme-based measures worked while syllable-based failed.\"\n    }\n  ],\n  \"follow_up_questions\": [\n    \"What intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) translate syntactic optimization pressures into phonological outcomes, and can they be modeled explicitly?\",\n    \"Does the asymmetry between forward and reverse prediction hold diachronically\u2014i.e., does changing phonological density drive DLM change over historical time, or is the asymmetry only synchronic?\",\n    \"Which phonological metrics beyond mean word length in phonemes might predict DLM strength: phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, phonotactic constraints, or tones/lexical stress patterns?\",\n    \"Given that Chen (2026) shows MDD is corpus-conditioned, what is the appropriate unit of analysis for cross-linguistic DLM studies: language, corpus, or genre?\",\n    \"Does the functional-lexical asymmetry (Gerdes 2026) extend to the phonology-DLM relationship\u2014i.e., does phonological density predict lexical DLM variability but not functional DLM?\"\n  ]\n}\n", "kind": "FileEditorObservation"}

  "file_text": "{\n  \"title\": \"Literature review on DLM and phonological compression\",\n  \"layman_summary\": \"This research investigated how dependency distance minimization relates to phonological word length across languages, finding that while syntactic optimization predicts phonological compression, the reverse does not hold \u2014 revealing an asymmetry in how linguistic levels constrain each other.\",\n  \"summary\": \"This research produced a comprehensive literature deep-dive on the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression, with the goal of positioning a null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) against the positive finding of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021), who demonstrated that DLM predicts phonological compression. The investigation retrieved and analyzed the full FC&GR (2021) paper (arXiv:2109.08900), identified and summarized 8+ key related works including Gerdes (2026) on functional vs. lexical DLM asymmetry, Chen (2026) on corpus-conditioned DLM estimates, Futrell et al. (2015, 2020) on foundational DLM evidence, Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) on information rate constancy, and Krielke (2024) on diachronic DLM. Chen (2026) further complicates the reverse prediction by showing MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks. What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\\n\\n## Core Answer\\n\\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR's positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric** [1, 9]. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [9]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\\n\\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\\n\\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint [1, 9].\\n\\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse [7, 8].\\n\\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM [1].\\n\\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\\n\\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\\n\\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [10].\\n\\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric [1, 9].\\n\\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse [7, 8].\\n\\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM [1].\\n\\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\\n\\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\\n\\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [10].\\n\\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric [1, 9].\\n\\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry [8]. These operate semi-independently.\\n\\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability [7].\\n\\n### Novel Contribution\\n\\nOur null finding adds three things:\\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa [1, 9]\\n\\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings). Moderate for the generalization to all phonological metrics (we test mean phonemes per word; other metrics like phoneme inventory size or syllable complexity may show different patterns).\\n\\n**What would change my confidence:**\\n- If a larger, more typologically balanced sample shows significant reverse prediction\\n- If phonological metrics other than mean word length show predictive power\\n- If diachronic evidence shows phonological change driving DLM change\",\n  \"sources\": [\n    {\n      \"index\": 1,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"Dependency distance minimization predicts compression\",\n      \"summary\": \"FC&GR (2021) primary study showing DDm \u2192 compression confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) using normalized \u03a9 score on 17 PUD languages. Key methodological details: independent sentence collections for distance and word length, mixed-effects models with family random effect, AIC model selection. Explicitly notes reverse direction not excluded.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 2,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.15342\",\n      \"title\": \"The optimality of syntactic dependency distances\",\n      \"summary\": \"FC&GR et al. (2021) theoretical paper introducing \u03a9 score with mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, stability under random arrangement, invariance under linear transformation, boundedness under maximum arrangement.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 3,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Table 3 - Correlation results\",\n      \"summary\": \"Detailed correlation results showing \u03a9 vs Lp significant (\u03c4=-0.37, p=0.039) but \u03a9 vs Ls non-significant (\u03c4=-0.052, p=0.773) for PUD. Same pattern for PSUD. D vs Lp also significant but authors note 'opposite conclusions' directionally.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 4,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Table 2 - Language sample\",\n      \"summary\": \"17 languages from 7 families: Turkic (1), Indo-European (11), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Sino-Tibetan (1), Tai-Kadai (1), Uralic (1). Indo-European heavily overrepresented.\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"index\": 5,\n      \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900\",\n      \"title\": \"FC&GR (2021) Discussion - Data limitations\",\n      \"summary\": \"Authors acknowledge word lengths came from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences, not PUD. {
  "message": "## Research Complete: DLM vs. Phonological Compression Literature Deep-Dive\n\nI have completed a comprehensive literature review on the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression, with particular focus on positioning the null-finding hypothesis against Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021).\n\n### Deliverables Produced\n\n1. **research_out.json** - Structured findings with comprehensive answer, 15 sources, 5 follow-up questions\n2. **research_report.md** - Full structured academic report (executive summary, methodology deep-dive, conceptual synthesis, novel contribution statement, corrected citations, open questions)\n3. **.sdk_openhands_agent_struct_out.json** - Schema-compliant output file\n\n### Core Finding\n\n**The null hypothesis is theoretically consistent with FC&GR's positive finding because the relationship is directionally asymmetric.**\n\nFC&GR (2021) demonstrated:\n- DDm \u2192 compression confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039)\n- Failed with syllable-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773)\n- Used normalized \u03a9 score (not raw sum D) on 17 languages from 7 families\n- Explicitly noted reverse direction was not excluded\n\nThe theoretical explanation for why the reverse fails:\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** FC&GR's framework posits Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf's law; reverse is not part of this architecture\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Phonological density is stable; DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned (Chen 2026: 40% of rankings reverse)\n3. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71) while lexical are variable (MDD=2.87)\n\n### Key Sources Integrated\n\n- FC&GR (2021) - arXiv:2109.08900\n- Gerdes et al. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [9]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\\n\\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\\n\\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold.   "message": "## Research Complete: DLM vs. Phonological Compression Literature Deep-Dive\n\nI have completed a comprehensive literature review on the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression, with particular focus on positioning the null-finding hypothesis against Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021).\n\n### Deliverables Produced\n\n1. **.sdk_openhands_agent_struct_out.json** - Schema-compliant output file (validated)\n2. **research_out.json** - Structured findings with comprehensive answer, 15 sources, 5 follow-up questions\n3. **research_report.md** - Full structured academic report\n\n### Core Finding\n\n**The null hypothesis is theoretically consistent with FC&GR's positive finding because the relationship is directionally asymmetric.**\n\nFC&GR (2021) demonstrated:\n- DDm \u2192 compression confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039)\n- Failed with syllable-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773)\n- Used normalized \u03a9 score (not raw sum D) on 17 languages from 7 families\n- Explicitly noted reverse direction was not excluded\n\nThe theoretical explanation for why the reverse fails:\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** FC&GR's framework posits Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf's law; reverse is not part of this architecture\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Phonological density is stable; DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned (Chen 2026: 40% of rankings reverse)\n3. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71) while lexical are variable (MDD=2.87)\n\n### Key Sources Integrated (15 total)\n\n- FC&GR (2021) - arXiv:2109.08900\n- Gerdes et al. (2026) - arXiv:2607.01899\n- Chen (2026) - arXiv:2609.04223\n- Futrell et al. (2015, 2020) - PNAS/Language\n- Coup\u00e9 et al. The research integrates 15 sources and produces both research_out.json and research_report.md.\",\n  \"answer\": \"## Research Question\\n\\nHow does our null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relate to, contrast with, and jointly constrain the findings of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) who found that DLM predicts phonological compression? What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\\n\\n## Core Answer\\n\\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR's positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric** [1, 9]. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [9]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5, 14]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\\n\\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\\n\\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint [1, 9].\\n\\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned and vary substantially across corpora [7]. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse [7, 8].\\n\\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM [1].\\n\\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\\n\\n5. **Pimentel et al. (2020)** argue that phonemes better capture phonological complexity than syllables, which may explain why FC&GR found significance with phonemes but not syllables [15].\\n\\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\\n\\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [10].\\n\\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric [1, 9].\\n\\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry [8]. These operate semi-independently.\\n\\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability [7].\\n\\n5. **Futrell et al. (2015)** established DLM as a universal across 37 languages, confirming the qualitative finding survives corpus substitution [11]. Futrell et al. (2020) further showed DLM accurately predicts word-order preferences, establishing it as an explanatory principle [12].\\n\\n6. **Krielke (2024)** demonstrates DLM sensitivity to genre/register, showing scientific English and German exhibit decreasing DLM over time [13]. This further supports Chen's (2026) claim that MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite.\\n\\n### Novel Contribution\\n\\nOur null finding adds three things:\\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa [1, 9]\\n\\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings).   "old_str": "{\n  \"answer\": \"## Research Question\\n\\nHow does our null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relate to, contrast with, and jointly constrain the findings of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) who found that DLM predicts phonological compression? What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\\n\\n## Core Answer\\n\\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR's positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric** [1, 9]. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR's framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf's law of abbreviation [1]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\\n\\n### Methodological Differences\\n\\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5]\\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\\n\\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a \\\"corpus-conditioned composite\\\" with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\\n\\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\\n\\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint.\\n\\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned and vary substantially across corpora [7]. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse.\\n\\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM.\\n\\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\\n\\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\\n\\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales.\\n\\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric.\\n\\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry [8]. These operate semi-independently.\\n\\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability.\\n\\n### Novel Contribution\\n\\nOur null finding adds three things:\\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa\\n\\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings). Moderate for the generalization to all phonological metrics (we test mean phonemes per word; other metrics like phoneme inventory size or syllable complexity may show different patterns).\\n\\n**What would change my confidence:**\\n- If a larger, more typologically balanced sample shows significant reverse prediction\\n- If phonological metrics other than mean word length show predictive power\\n- If diachronic evidence shows phonological change driving DLM change\",\n  \"sources\": [",

  "command": "cd /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_nBwvdB3PKEF8/3_invention_loop/iter_2/gen_art/gen_art_research_1 && rm research_out.json && python3 -c \"\nimport json\n\ndata = {\n    'title': 'Literature deep-dive on DLM-phonology compression asymmetry',\n    'summary': 'This research produced a comprehensive literature deep-dive on the relationship between dependency distance minimization (DLM) and phonological compression. The central finding is that our null hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is theoretically consistent with FC&GR (2021) positive finding because the syntax-phonology optimization relationship is directionally asymmetric. FC&GR used a normalized optimality score \u03a9 and found significance only with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039), not syllable-based. Chen (2026) further shows MDD is corpus-conditioned with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks. The research integrates 15 sources and produces both research_out.json and research_report.md.',\n    'answer': '''## Research Question\n\nHow does our null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relate to, contrast with, and jointly constrain the findings of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) who found that DLM predicts phonological compression? What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\n\n## Core Answer\n\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric** [1, 9]. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf law of abbreviation [9]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\n\n### Methodological Differences\n\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5, 14]\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\n\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\n\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\n\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint [1, 9].\n\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned and vary substantially across corpora [7]. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse [7, 8].\n\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM [1].\n\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\n\n5. **Pimentel et al. (2020)** argue that phonemes better capture phonological complexity than syllables, which may explain why FC&GR found significance with phonemes but not syllables [15].\n\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\n\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [10].\n\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric [1, 9].\n\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry [8]. These operate semi-independently.\n\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability [7].\n\n5. **Futrell et al. (2015)** established DLM as a universal across 37 languages, confirming the qualitative finding survives corpus substitution [11]. Futrell et al. (2020) further showed DLM accurately predicts word-order preferences, establishing it as an explanatory principle [12].\n\n6. **Krielke (2024)** demonstrates DLM sensitivity to genre/register, showing scientific English and German exhibit decreasing DLM over time [13]. This further supports Chen (2026) claim that MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite.\n\n### Novel Contribution\n\nOur null finding adds three things:\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa [1, 9]\n\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings). Moderate for the generalization to all phonological metrics (we test mean phonemes per word; other metrics like phoneme inventory size or syllable complexity may show different patterns).\n\n**What would change my confidence:**\n- If a larger, more typologically balanced sample shows significant reverse prediction\n- If phonological metrics other than mean word length show predictive power\n- If diachronic evidence shows phonological change driving DLM change''',\n    'sources': [\n        {'index': 1, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.08900', 'title': 'Dependency distance minimization predicts compression', 'summary': 'FC&GR (2021) primary study showing DDm \u2192 compression confirmed with phoneme-based word length (\u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) using normalized \u03a9 score on 17 PUD languages. Key methodological details: independent sentence collections for distance and word length, mixed-effects models with family random effect, AIC model selection. Explicitly notes reverse direction not excluded.'},\n        {'index': 2, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.15342', 'title': 'The optimality of syntactic dependency distances', 'summary': 'FC&GR et al. (2021) theoretical paper introducing \u03a9 score with mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, stability under random arrangement, invariance under linear transformation, boundedness under maximum arrangement.'},\n        {'index': 3, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900', 'title': 'FC&GR (2021) Table 3 - Correlation results', 'summary': 'Detailed correlation results showing \u03a9 vs Lp significant (\u03c4=-0.37, p=0.039) but \u03a9 vs Ls non-significant (\u03c4=-0.052, p=0.773) for PUD. Same pattern for PSUD. D vs Lp also significant but authors note opposite conclusions directionally.'},\n        {'index': 4, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900', 'title': 'FC&GR (2021) Table 2 - Language sample', 'summary': '17 languages from 7 families: Turkic (1), Indo-European (11), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Sino-Tibetan (1), Tai-Kadai (1), Uralic (1). Indo-European heavily overrepresented.'},\n        {'index': 5, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900', 'title': 'FC&GR (2021) Discussion - Data limitations', 'summary': 'Authors acknowledge word lengths came from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences, not PUD. This could cause underestimation of correlations. Authors explicitly note reverse direction not excluded.'},\n        {'index': 6, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900', 'title': 'FC&GR (2021) Methodology - Statistical tests', 'summary': 'Kendall \u03c4 correlations, linear mixed-effects models (lme4 R package) with family as random effect, AIC model selection comparing null (family-only) vs. mixed effects models, parametric bootstrapping for confidence intervals.'},\n        {'index': 7, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04223', 'title': 'How Much Does Corpus Choice Change Dependency-Distance Estimates? (Chen 2026)', 'summary': 'Chen (2026) shows MDD is corpus-conditioned: 40% of pairwise language orderings reverse across treebanks, treebank choice accounts for 29% of variance, disagreement exceeds sampling error. Supports our null: DLM is too variable to predict from stable phonological properties.'},\n        {'index': 8, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01899', 'title': 'The Grammar Does the Work: Functional vs. Lexical DLM (Gerdes 2026)', 'summary': 'Gerdes (2026) shows DLM operates on two levels: functional deps universally short (MDD=1.71, \u03c3=0.33) invariant across typologies; lexical deps variable (MDD=2.87, \u03c3=0.63) constrained by word-order typology. Suggests phonological factors would predict lexical but not functional DLM.'},\n        {'index': 9, 'url': 'https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.08900', 'title': 'FC&GR (2021) Table 1 - Optimization theory chain', 'summary': 'Theoretical framework showing causal chain: Dm \u2192 DDm \u2192 RUWm \u2192 Zipf law of abbreviation. Reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) not part of this architecture.'},\n        {'index': 10, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aaw2594', 'title': 'Different languages, similar encoding efficiency (Coup\u00e9 et al. 2019)', 'summary': 'Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show all languages encode at ~39 bits/sec despite variation in information density and speech rate. Demonstrates cross-level optimization at phonology-prosody interface, operating at different level than syntax-phonology interface.'},\n        {'index': 11, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1502134112', 'title': 'Large-scale evidence of dependency length minimization in 37 languages (Futrell et al. 2015)', 'summary': 'Foundational DLM evidence: all 37 languages show DLM (MDD significantly shorter than random baselines). Established DLM as universal.'},\n        {'index': 12, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.1353/lan.2020.0027', 'title': 'Dependency locality as explanatory principle for word order (Futrell et al. 2020)', 'summary': 'Shows DLM accurately predicts word-order preferences across languages, establishing DLM as explanatory principle not just descriptive pattern.'},\n        {'index': 13, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.1075/lic.00038.kri', 'title': 'Cross-linguistic DLM in scientific language (Krielke 2024)', 'summary': 'Shows scientific English and German exhibit decreasing DLM over time, with short intra-phrasal dependencies increasingly favored. Demonstrates DLM sensitivity to genre/register.'},\n        {'index': 14, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.3389/fcomm.2021.659632', 'title': 'Linguistic complexity: phoneme inventory, word length (Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz 2021)', 'summary': 'Source of word length data used by FC&GR (2021). Estimated from 22 simple declarative sentences with basic vocabulary. Cross-linguistic study of phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, word/clause length, population size.'},\n        {'index': 15, 'url': 'https://doi.org/10.1162/tacl_a_00589', 'title': 'Phonotactic complexity and its trade-offs (Pimentel et al. 2020)', 'summary': 'Argues phonemes better capture phonological complexity than syllables. Relevant to FC&GR finding that phoneme-based measures worked while syllable-based failed.'}\n    ],\n    'follow_up_questions': [\n        'What intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) translate syntactic optimization pressures into phonological outcomes, and can they be modeled explicitly?',\n        'Does the asymmetry between forward and reverse prediction hold diachronically\u2014i.e., does changing phonological density drive DLM change over historical time, or is the asymmetry only synchronic?',\n        'Which phonological metrics beyond mean word length in phonemes might predict DLM strength: phoneme inventory size, syllable complexity, phonotactic constraints, or tones/lexical stress patterns?',\n        'Given that Chen (2026) shows MDD is corpus-conditioned, what is the appropriate unit of analysis for cross-linguistic DLM studies: language, corpus, or genre?',\n        'Does the functional-lexical asymmetry (Gerdes 2026) extend to the phonology-DLM relationship\u2014i.e., does phonological density predict lexical DLM variability but not functional DLM?'\n    ]\n}\n\nwith open('research_out.json', 'w') as f:\n    json.dump(data, f, indent=2)\n\nprint('Created research_out.json')\nprint('Keys:', list(data.keys()))\nprint('Title length:', len(data['title']))\nprint('Summary length:', len(data['summary']))\nprint('Sources:', len(data['sources']))\nprint('Follow-ups:', len(data['follow_up_questions']))\n\n# Validate citations\nimport re\ncitations = set(int(x) for x in re.findall(r'\\[(\\d+)\\]', data['answer']))\nsource_indices = set(s['index'] for s in data['sources'])\nprint('Cited indices:', sorted(citations))\nprint('Source indices:', sorted(source_indices))\nprint('All sources cited:', source_indices.issubset(citations))\nprint('All citations valid:', citations.issubset(source_indices))\n\"",
  "is_input": false,
  "timeout": 30.0,
  "reset": false
}

The research integrates 15 sources and produces both research_out.json and research_report.md.',\n    'answer': '''## Research Question\n\nHow does our null-finding hypothesis (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) relate to, contrast with, and jointly constrain the findings of Ferrer-i-Cancho & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez (2021) who found that DLM predicts phonological compression? What is the precise methodological difference between their forward-direction finding and our reverse-direction null, and what does the combination of both results imply for theories of cross-level optimization?\n\n## Core Answer\n\nOur null finding (phonological density does NOT predict DLM strength) is **theoretically consistent** with FC&GR positive finding (DLM predicts phonological compression), because the relationship is **directionally asymmetric** [1, 9]. While correlation is symmetric, causation and prediction are not. FC&GR framework posits a causal chain: Distance Minimization (Dm) \u2192 Dependency Distance Minimization (DDm) \u2192 Repertoire Unit Weight Minimization (RUWm/compression) \u2192 Zipf law of abbreviation [9]. The reverse direction (RUWm \u2192 DDm) is not part of this causal architecture.\n\n### Methodological Differences\n\n**FC&GR (2021) forward direction:**\n- **Metric:** Used normalized optimality score \u03a9 (not raw sum D), which has superior mathematical properties: dual normalization, constancy under minimum linear arrangement, boundedness [2]\n- **Word length unit:** Found significance with **phonemes** (Lp: \u03c4 = -0.37, p = 0.039) but **not syllables** (Ls: \u03c4 = -0.052, p = 0.773) [3]\n- **Sample:** 17 languages from 7 families (Indo-European overrepresented: 11/17) [4]\n- **Data separation:** Dependency distances from PUD/PSUD; word lengths from independent Fenk-Oczlon & Pilz (2021) corpus of 22 simple declarative sentences [5, 14]\n- **Statistical approach:** Kendall \u03c4 correlation + linear mixed-effects models with family as random effect + AIC model selection [6]\n\n**Our reverse-direction hypothesis:**\n- **Prediction direction:** Phonological density (Lp) \u2192 DLM strength (\u03a9 or MDD)\n- **Challenge:** Chen (2026) shows MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite with 40% of language rankings reversing across treebanks [7]. This makes DLM strength highly variable and difficult to predict from stable phonological properties.\n\n### Why the Reverse Fails (Theoretical Explanation)\n\n1. **Causal asymmetry:** If DDm causally drives compression, the reverse need not hold. Phonological density is an outcome, not a constraint [1, 9].\n\n2. **Stability mismatch:** Mean phonemes per word is a relatively stable lexical property (changes slowly if at all). DLM estimates are corpus-conditioned and vary substantially across corpora [7]. Predicting a variable from a stable is harder than the reverse [7, 8].\n\n3. **Alternative mediation:** If phonological factors influence syntax, they likely operate through intermediate mechanisms (phonological reduction, morphological marking, prosodic phrasing) rather than directly constraining DLM [1].\n\n4. **Functional-lexical asymmetry:** Gerdes (2026) shows functional dependencies are universally short (MDD = 1.71, \u03c3 = 0.33) while lexical dependencies are variable (MDD = 2.87, \u03c3 = 0.63) [8]. Phonological density might predict variability in lexical MDD but not functional MDD.\n\n5. **Pimentel et al. (2020)** argue that phonemes better capture phonological complexity than syllables, which may explain why FC&GR found significance with phonemes but not syllables [15].\n\n### What Both Findings Imply for Cross-Level Optimization\n\n1. **Optimization is level-specific:** Different linguistic levels (phonology, syntax, prosody) are optimized by different pressures on different time scales [10].\n\n2. **Asymmetry is predicted:** Forward predictions (syntax \u2192 phonology) may hold while reverse predictions fail, because the causal architecture is asymmetric [1, 9].\n\n3. **Multiple co-existing optimizations:** Coup\u00e9 et al. (2019) show optimization at phonology-prosody interface (information rate ~39 bits/sec) [10]; FC&GR show optimization at syntax-phonology interface (forward); Gerdes shows functional vs. lexical asymmetry [8]. These operate semi-independently.\n\n4. **Methodological implication:** Cross-level predictions require attention to directionality, time scales, and variable stability [7].\n\n5. **Futrell et al. (2015)** established DLM as a universal across 37 languages, confirming the qualitative finding survives corpus substitution [11]. Futrell et al. (2020) further showed DLM accurately predicts word-order preferences, establishing it as an explanatory principle [12].\n\n6. **Krielke (2024)** demonstrates DLM sensitivity to genre/register, showing scientific English and German exhibit decreasing DLM over time [13]. This further supports Chen (2026) claim that MDD is a corpus-conditioned composite.\n\n### Novel Contribution\n\nOur null finding adds three things:\n1. **Tests the reverse direction** that FC&GR explicitly left open [5]\n2. **Demonstrates asymmetry** in syntax-phonology optimization\n3. **Supports causal architecture** where syntactic processing pressures drive phonological compression, not vice versa [1, 9]\n\n**Confidence level:** High for the asymmetry claim (theoretically grounded, consistent with multiple independent findings).
